                                 1. GUIDE TO THE SUPPLEMENT
7                                                                                               7

8      The opening sections prove the costate, performance, and observation results used        8

9    in the main article and report all primary and fixed-work outcomes. The retained           9

10   sections give the complete application-specific arguments and earlier numerical            10

11   evidence. A finite-game, finite-grid, or scalar-reference statement retains its stated     11

12   domain; each continuous-time capital statement uses its own transfer account.              12

13                                                                                              13
                     2. ADDITIONAL PROOFS AND NUMERICAL CONTRACTS

1    decomposition. If the conditional Euler costate differs from the continuous policy       1

2    costate by b( X ), that term remains an additional bias; it is not removed by the        2

3    projection identity. Neither the sampled costate loss nor a successful optimizer         3

4    return is a uniform upper bound on this bias.                                            4

5                                                                                             5

6                     2.2. Cancellation, clipping and finite-population inference             6

7                                                                                             7
         For each candidate, the exact payoff identity used in Proposition 8 consists of
8                                                                                             8
     a policy production term, its consumption term, its terminal dispersion, and the
9                                                                                             9
     corresponding reference terms. When two candidates use the identical reference
10                                                                                            10
     path, subtraction cancels every exact reference term. Comparing the two policy
11                                                                                            11
     terms with their numerical approximations therefore requires the two policy transfer
12                                                                                            12
     errors and the statistic-roundoff accounts, but no reference-state transfer error. The
13                                                                                            13
     implementation retains conservative original statistic budgets even where their
14                                                                                            14
     reference quadrature components also cancel.
15                                                                                            15
         For any real x, y and positive thresholds ba , bb ,
16                                                                                            16

17                       (| x − y| − ba − bb )+ ≤ (| x | − ba )+ + (|y| − bb )+ .             17

18                                                                                            18

19   Taking expectations proves the summed clipping-tail allowance without requiring          19

20   independence between policies. Applying the empirical Bernstein inequality to the        20

21   clipped difference and then to its negative gives the two one-sided bounds. A union      21

22   bound covers all declared tests. Our allocation counts both directions and every         22

23   method, profile design and auxiliary evaluation; it does not spend new confidence        23

24   mass for deterministic transformations such as (58).                                     24

25       For the finite population, draw I with the fixed probabilities p j , independently   25

26   of each future innovation path. The pair ( I, W ) is then independent across paths. 26
27   Conditional transfer gives                                                               27

28                                                                                            28

29                       E[ Xa,h ] − ∑ p j { Jy j ( a) − Jy j (m0 )} ≤ ∑ p j Ea,h (y j ).     29
                                       j                                 j
30                                                                                            30

31   The clipping threshold is the maximum of the profile-specific thresholds; its            31

32   expectation-tail allowance is bounded by the maximum of their allowances. The            32
                                       NBO SUPPLEMENT                                     3

1    prescribed implementation uses equal probabilities over nine fixed profiles. Both         1

2    averaging and the final subtraction are interval-enclosed. Random empirical pro- 2
3    file frequencies are not substituted for the population weights when calculating          3

4    deterministic error budgets. They are part of the sampling variation of the final         4

5    statistic.                                                                                5

6      For the paired method difference, the same profile is assigned to both policies on      6

7    each path. Common random numbers across different methods or training seeds               7

8    do not invalidate a finite-family union bound. They also do not create independent        8

9    replications of the optimizer. The ten fixed training streams are reported individually   9

10   and descriptively; no confidence assertion about an unspecified distribution of           10

11   future hyperparameter choices is inferred.                                                11

12                                                                                             12

13                           2.3. Observed-history implementation                              13

14                                                                                             14
       Under the observation assumptions, M is a continuous martingale with bracket
15                                                                                             15
     ⟨ M⟩t = Qt. Let A = Σ⊤ Q−1 . The matrix AΣ is the orthogonal projector onto the row
16                                                                                             16
     space of Σ, and nn⊤ is the projector onto its orthogonal complement. Independence
17                                                                                             17
     of the private Brownian motion gives
18                                                                                             18

19                        ⟨ AM + nB0 ⟩t = ( AQA⊤ + nn⊤ )t = Id+1 t.                            19

20                                                                                             20

21   The multivariate Lévy characterization proves the Brownian assertion. Also ΣA = Id        21

22   and Σn = 0, so ΣW e = M. On each decision cell, the action is determined at its left      22

23   endpoint by the stored internal state and past recovered increments. With that held       23

24   action, the physical state equation has a unique strong solution because the produc- 24
25   tion drift is globally Lipschitz and bounded. Induction over the cells establishes        25

26   uniqueness in law for the joint physical and internal process. Driven by the Brown- 26
27   ian motion just constructed, it therefore has the same law as the controller originally   27

28   defined using directly supplied innovations. Applying the specified componentwise         28

29   clipping to the recovered vector preserves this distributional equivalence.               29

30     The proposition uses only private randomization and observed state history; it          30

31   does not reveal the original null-space economic driver. Randomized controls are          31

32   admissible in the enlarged filtration. Since the original deterministic performance       32
     4

1    envelope holds against all such adapted controls and independent randomization                    1

2    cannot improve an unconstrained expected-payoff supremum beyond the best                          2

3    deterministic randomization realization, the comparison with the original optimum                 3

4    is preserved. The result is not an implementability theorem for discrete noisy state              4

5    measurements. The exact drift integral is stated explicitly, and errors in approximat- 5
6    ing it must be treated separately.                                                                6

7                                                                                                      7

8                                  2.4. Independent scalar HJB approximation                           8

9                                                                                                      9
         For d = 1, the terminal dispersion penalty vanishes but production remains
10                                                                                                     10
     nonlinear in log capital. The reference solves the same primitive action-box problem,
11                                                                                                     11
     not a Riccati or homothetic reduction. Put f (y) = κ tanh( By) and ν = (σI2 + σC2 )/2.
12                                                                                                     12
     On a uniform grid of width ∆y, define the positive rates
13                                                                                                     13

14                          ν    (m − c − f (yi ))+                     ν    ( c + f ( yi ) − m )+     14
              ℓi ( m ) =       +                    ,     ri ( m ) =       +                       .
15                         ∆y2          ∆y                             ∆y2             ∆y              15

16                                                                                                     16
     The implicit Bellman step solves
17                                                                                                     17
      vi − vi+
18
                  + ρvi = max{log m + yi − ζm2 /2 + ℓi (m)(vi−1 − vi ) + ri (m)(vi+1 − vi )}.          18

19
          h                    m
                                                                                                       19

20   For a fixed action vector the coefficient matrix is strictly diagonally dominant with             20

21   positive diagonal and nonpositive off-diagonal entries. Its inverse is nonnegative. 21
22   Howard updates maximize over each drift-sign interval; on either interval the                     22

23   positive root of 1/m − ζm = p is clipped to its feasible endpoints, and the two                   23

24   candidates are compared. This is an independent monotone matrix computation,                      24

25   not a trained critic used as its own reference.                                                   25

26       At the two outer boundaries, the prescribed affine asymptotes replace produc- 26
27   tion by its limiting saturation value. If w(t) is the coefficient on initial log capital          27

28   and m0 (t) the common schedule, their intercept is the numerical integral of the                  28

29   corresponding deterministic discounted flow. The reference reports the grid, time                 29

30   step, boundary distance, linear-system residual, policy-iteration convergence and                 30

31   domain sensitivity. Fitted actors are evaluated as Markov feedbacks on this finite                31

32   grid. These comparisons do not conflate that controller with the innovation-driven                32
                                         NBO SUPPLEMENT                                    5

1    implementation. Grid differences and asymptotic boundary values are not asserted          1

2    to be rigorous continuous-domain error enclosures.                                        2

3                                                                                              3

4                            2.5. Reproducibility and cost accounting                          4

5      The primary fitting budget includes model initialization, optimizer creation, roll- 5
6    outs, derivative calculations, updates, validation and checkpoint writes. A process       6

7    completes its current iteration and records the resulting overshoot. The common           7

8    numerical-runtime warm-up precedes the clock for every method. Method order               8

9    is rotated across the ten independent streams. Checkpoint frontiers choose saved          9

10   objects by elapsed fitting work, before any final simulation result is inspected. Every   10

11   final frontier row receives the same full path count and transfer calculation as a        11

12   selected primary policy.                                                                  12

13     Wall-clock stopping is hardware-dependent. The ledger consequently retains the          13

14   realized iteration count, simulator-state visits, optimization steps, peak process        14

15   memory, all checkpoints, selected checkpoint and source hash. Re-executing the            15

16   time-budget protocol reproduces the experimental design, not a promise of bitwise         16

17   identical weights on a different processor. A fixed-iteration replay can use the saved    17

18   counts and the same random streams to investigate numerical discrepancies. The            18

19   numerical compiler, random generator and machine arithmetic remain subject to             19

20   the inherited conditional contract.                                                       20

22        5. INDEPENDENT SCALAR REFERENCE: EQUATIONS AND COMPLETE DIAGNOSTICS                         22

23                                                                                                    23
                          5.1. The scalar reference and its residual account
24                                                                                                    24

25     The scalar reference isolates policy evaluation and policy improvement in a                    25

26   nonlinear member of the coupled-capital family. It uses the three stored scalar actors           26

27   selected in the original experiment and the analytical anchor, without retraining. The           27

28   four state–time–domain specifications are (401, 256, 6), (801, 512, 6), (1601, 1024, 6)          28

29   and (1601, 1024, 8), where the last coordinate is the half-width L of the log-capital            29

30   interval. All model coefficients, action limits, terminal values and boundary data               30

31   remain those of the original reference. The diagnostic program checks the source and             31

32   input hashes before computation and verifies the input hashes again afterwards.                  32
                                            NBO SUPPLEMENT                                     27

     The original cumulative-trapezoid rule and time interpolation evaluate these func- 10
11                                                                                                11

12                                                                                                12

13                                                                                                13

14                                                                                                14

16                                                                                                16

19
     For a fixed policy, omit the maximization and use its stored feedback action. The            19

20
     diagnostic files retain the maximum residual at every time step, the linear residual         20

21
     of every Howard evaluation, every iterate difference, and the iteration count. The           21

22
     original stopping rule is an iterate difference below 10−11 , with a cap of 100 itera- 22
23
     tions. A separate Howard check evaluates ∥Tk (vk ) − vk ∥∞ /h, where Tk solves the           23

24
     linear system for the greedy policy selected from vk .                                       24

25
       The significance of these residuals can be stated directly. Let ve be the exact solution   25

26
     of the same finite-grid backward equations, with the same frozen policy (or the same         26

27
     Bellman operator), terminal data and boundary data, and let v be the approximate             27

28
     sequence whose defect is r. In exact arithmetic, the fixed-policy matrix has positive        28

29
     diagonal, nonpositive off-diagonal entries, and row sums at least h−1 + ρ after              29

30
     boundary elimination. The discrete maximum principle therefore yields                        30

31                                                   ∥vek+1 − vk+1 ∥∞ + hRk                       31
                                   ∥vek − vk ∥∞ ≤

1    whenever the boundary data agree and ∥r k ∥∞ ≤ Rk . The same estimate holds for the       1

2    Bellman step: at a positive maximum of the difference, select an action maximizing        2

3    the Hamiltonian of the upper candidate and apply the fixed-action maximum                 3

4    principle; interchange the candidates for the negative part. With exact common            4

5    terminal data this gives                                                                  5

                                n t −1                     max Rk
                                          hRk

                                                       ≤    k

                                k=0 (1 + ρh )
                                                                ρ
8                                                                                              8

9    The archived propagation diagnostic substitutes the observed floating-point resid- 9
10   uals in this formula. These residuals are not outward-rounded enclosures, so that         10

11   substitution is a numerical diagnostic rather than a certified bound. The estimate        11

12   concerns the finite-grid equations with the prescribed boundary data; it does not         12

13   include consistency, state interpolation or boundary bias.                                13

14     The tables report all four grids, the five initial states y0 ∈ {−1, −0.5, 0, 0.5, 1},   14

15   the policies’ action-bound frequencies, and changes in values, optimal actions and        15

16   policy losses. Initial-state values are linearly interpolated from nodal time-zero        16

17   values. Bound frequencies use an absolute tolerance of 10−12 and count equally            17

18   weighted interior state–time nodes; they are not occupation probabilities. The            18

19   first two grid comparisons halve both h and δ. Increasing L from 6 to 8 at fixed          19

20   (ny , nt ) = (1601, 1024) changes δ from 0.0075 to 0.01. This comparison measures         20

21   joint domain and spacing sensitivity, not boundary truncation error in isolation. 21
22   Full common-domain changes and their locations accompany the central-region               22

23   summaries.                                                                                23

24     The scalar calculation supplies an independent finite-grid benchmark for non- 24
25   linear feedback, with a reproducible algebraic error account. In the 10-, 20- and         25

26   50-dimensional economies, cross-sectional dispersion, the coupling matrix and the         26

27   covariance structure remain economically active. Moreover, this scalar benchmark          27

28   evaluates Markov feedback, whereas the continuous-time deployment theorem                 28

29   specifies a randomized controller with innovation history. Scalar reference losses        29

30   therefore have their stated one-dimensional meaning; multidimensional conclu- 30
31   sions rely on the separate multidimensional policy comparisons and certification          31

32   arguments.                                                                                32

21     The scalar weights Ak , Bk are integrated from their exponential expressions. Ck ,          21

                            8.3. Arithmetic contract and forward-error budget                      30

31     All stored binary64 model coefficients denote exact real inputs to the economic             31

32   problem. The computation assumes round-to-nearest elementary arithmetic, the                  32
     36

1    usual dot-product forward-error bound, and correctly rounded input conversion. It       1

2    does not assume correct rounding of a platform exponential, logarithm, or hyper- 2
3    bolic tangent when proving a payoff endpoint. Network library functions produce         3

4    proposals only; the interval schedule and inward projection enforce feasibility         4

5    independently of proposal accuracy. The simulation drift and log utility use the        5

6    polynomial kernels described next. The Python interpreter, numerical libraries, com- 6
7    piler, hardware, and random-number generator are not machine-formally verified. 7
8      For | x | ≤ 20, write r = x/256 and approximate er by its degree-14 Taylor poly- 8
9    nomial in Horner form. Nine squarings approximate e2x . The Taylor remainder            9

10   is bounded by e|r| |r |15 /15!. The coefficient representation and Horner operations    10

11   satisfy the standard γ30 forward bound, with γ j = ju/(1 − ju) and u = 2−52 , a         11

12   conservative choice relative to binary64 unit roundoff. Propagating relative error      12

13   through nine squarings bounds the exponential relative error by less than 2−35 on       13

14   this range. The derivative of 1 − 2/(1 + z) with respect to log z is 2z/(1 + z)2 ≤ 1/2. 14
15   Including the final arithmetic and saturation outside [−20, 20] therefore bounds        15

16   absolute tanh error by 2−34 . The saturation error itself is below 2e−40 .              16

17     For positive consumption, binary exponent extraction writes x = 2e−1 (2m), with       17

18   m ∈ [1/2, 1). Then                                                                      18

19                                                                                           19
                           ∞   2j+1
                               q                              2m − 1
                log x = 2 ∑
20                                                                                           20
                                      + (e − 1) log 2,   q=          ∈ [0, 1/3].
21                        j =0
                               2j + 1                         2m + 1                         21

22                                                                                           22

23   The implementation retains 18 terms and evaluates log 2 with the same series at         23

24   q = 1/3. The geometric remainder and Horner forward error, including the bounded        24

25   exponents on the primitive consumption interval, are below 2−34 . No empirical          25

26   comparison with the platform logarithm serves as this error bound. The optional         26

27   compiled kernels use identical binary64 coefficients, operation order, and disabled     27

28   fast-math transformations; the array implementation is retained as a fallback.          28

29     Clipped innovations yield a deterministic internal state cap. A conservative cap      29

30   for the implemented ranges is                                                           30

                                                                 √

1    Using γd+2 Scap maxi ∑ j | Bij | for the matrix-product error, the code propagates drift,   1

2    innovation-combination, and state-addition errors through the same discrete Gron- 2
3    wall bound. It verifies the bootstrap condition that this enlargement is less than          3

4    one. This condition, together with the doubled cap, bounds the entire numerical             4

5    path. Separately, it accumulates the errors of all statistic sums, log evaluations,         5

6    and terminal squares. The exact formulas and intermediate constants are returned            6

7    by constants; each final record saves the resulting state cap, forward-roundoff             7

8    bound, scalar-integration bound, and total bias endpoint. Scalar transcendental             8

9    evaluations used only to assemble conservative budgets receive additional 10−12             9

10   bias and 10−10 clipping cushions on the declared bounded parameter range; they              10

11   do not replace the enclosed spectral or integral calculations.                              11

12     The sampling contract is ideal independent standard-normal innovations                    12

13   rounded to binary64 before the specified clipping, with the forward-error allowance         13

14   for their conversion. Pseudorandom draws are a reproducible implementation of               14

15   this contract, not a proof of ideal independence. The resulting certificate is condi- 15
16   tional numerical mathematics and conditional statistical inference. It must not be          16

17   described as a machine-formal guarantee of a particular hardware random stream. 17
18                                                                                               18

17     For completeness, the Gaussian concentration fact can be obtained from the Gaus- 17

                         8.5. Finite families, selection, and interpretation                       25

26     Conditioning on the fitted weights and their validation selection makes each final- 26
27   test statistic a fixed function of one fresh path. Each row is therefore an independent       27

28   sample across paths, even when its Brownian bank is shared with other policies. 28
29   Applying Maurer and Pontil (2009, Theorem 4) to both signs and taking a union                 29

30   bound proves the simultaneous statement. The implementation allocates 2,000                   30

31   one-sided statements, so at most 1,000 two-sided rows may be reported under this              31

32   allocation. The audit rejects an overrun. It also checks source weights, path counts,         32
                                       NBO SUPPLEMENT                                   39

1    raw-array hashes, every recomputed mean and variance, and all displayed table            1

2    inputs.                                                                                  2

3      A certificate may fail to establish improvement. That is distinct from an exception    3

4    in the numerical code. Both outcomes are retained. A zero correction, a deliber- 4
5    ately saturated poor correction, and trained corrections are compared under the          5

6    same accounting. Small training loss or successful completion is never substituted       6

7    for a positive lower improvement endpoint. If a candidate is chosen after inspect- 7
8    ing several final rows, the simultaneous guarantee continues to hold only within         8

9    the predeclared finite family; an undeclared search requires a new allocation and        9

10   independent data.                                                                        10

11                                                                                            11

12                                                                                            12

13                                                                                            13

14                              9. RELATION TO THE MAIN TEXT                                  14

15                                                                                            15

16     This supplement expands the models and algorithms of the revised main                  16

17   manuscript. It does not introduce an alternative total loss or a different equilibrium   17

18   definition. Policy evaluation freezes all actor outputs. Policy improvement freezes      18

19   the critic’s value and derivative channels. For games, only the acting player’s objec- 19
20   tive is differentiated. For temporal selves, both exponential components evaluate        20

21   the same future policy. The earlier main and supplementary sources are preserved         21

22   in the revision archive for comparison; they are not alternative implementations of      22

23   the revised method.                                                                      23

24     The inherited portfolio and quadratic laboratories retain their declared exact- 24
25   structure classes. R6 trains generic multilayer policies in stopped consumption,         25

26   preference adjustment, and dense nonlinear capital economies. R7 adds successful         26

27   finite preference actors, neural game policies with full dynamic best responses,         27

28   common-noise coverage records, and an independent arithmetic replay. R8 in- 28
29   tegrates those results and adds continuous-action training without maximizing            29

30   labels, complete-action enclosures, direct and projection comparisons, and a sepa- 30
31   rate continuous-transfer theorem. These approximation and verification classes are       31

32   distinguished throughout the supplement.                                                 32
     40

1                  10. POLICY EVALUATION AND THE RECURSIVE POLICY GRADIENT                                         1

2                                                                                                                  2
       Let aϕ be differentiable in a scalar parameter direction, and suppose V aϕ is suf-
3                                                                                                                  3
     ficiently differentiable for the following calculation. The terminal payoff is inde-
4                                                                                                                  4
     pendent of the policy parameter. Assume differentiation under the expectation
5                                                                                                                  5
     is justified, that the differentiated state and utility equations are integrable, and
6                                                                                                                  6
     that the boundary conditions of the differentiated problem are zero. Denote the
7                                                                                                                  7
     directional derivative of the policy value by w.
8                                                                                                                  8
       Differentiating the fixed-policy equation gives
9                                                                                                                  9

10                                                                                                                 10
                       0 = wt + L aϕ w + f y (t, x, aϕ , V aϕ )w + Da H aϕ [V aϕ ] Dϕ aϕ .
11                                                                                                                 11

12                                                                                                                 12
     The operator derivative includes the dependence of both drift and covariance on
13                                                                                                                 13
     the action. The derivative of the generator acting on the changing value is contained
14                                                                                                                 14
     in L aϕ w, while the action derivative of the generator acting on the fixed value is
15                                                                                                                 15
     contained in Da H. No Hessian-action channel is discarded.
16                                                                                                                 16
       Applying the linear Feynman–Kac formula to the differentiated equation gives
17                                                                                                                 17

18                          Z τ         Z s                                                                      18

19        w(t, x ) = Et,x         exp          f y (v, Sv , av , V )dv Da H aϕ [V aϕ ](s, Ss ) Dϕ aϕ (s, Ss )ds.
                                                               aϕ

20                                                                                                                 20

21   This formula concerns the performance derivative from the stated initial state. A                             21

22   domain-uniform Hamiltonian objective uses a different weighting. Such an objective                            22

23   is useful for approximate pointwise improvement, but it becomes a lifetime policy                             23

24   gradient only with the appropriate occupation and adjoint weights. Trajectories                               24

25   sampled by the current actor are detached in the implemented domain objective. Dif- 25
26   ferentiating through their sampling law would define a different gradient estimator                           26

27   and must be analyzed separately.                                                                              27

28     The critic’s residual derivative is the derivative of the entire fixed-policy equation. 28
29   In particular, its level is determined by the recursive evaluation equation together                          29

30   with boundary data. A separate negative-Hamiltonian gradient in the critic is not                             30

31   an innocuous level anchor: in the scalar stationary example it moves the minimizer                            31

32   away from the Bellman root.                                                                                   32

1          11. REPRESENTATION, BOUNDARY CONDITIONS, AND ERROR MEASUREMENT                        1

2                                                                                                2
                          11.1. Smooth approximation and what it establishes
3                                                                                                3

4
       A derivative-approximation result concerns a smooth target on a compact domain            4

5
     and a family of networks whose capacity can increase. It does not imply that a fixed        5

6
     network attains zero residual, that optimization finds its best approximation, or           6

7
     that a nonsmooth target has classical derivatives everywhere. In a smooth problem,          7

8
     increasing the representation class can reduce approximation error in the value and         8

9
     derivatives. In a nonsmooth problem, NBO instead evaluates a consistent positive- 9
10
     weight Bellman equation, or solves an explicitly regularized equation before taking         10

11
     a justified limit.                                                                          11

12
       The hard-terminal architecture is v(t, x ) = G ( x ) + ( T − t) N (t, x ). It exactly en- 12
13
     forces v( T, x ) = G ( x ) independently of training. A lateral Dirichlet face needs an     13

14
     additional compatible lifting and vanishing factor. For a reflected state, the Neu- 14
15
     mann condition is a different constraint. A loss on only interior points leaves all of      15

16
     these boundary questions unanswered. The regression test evaluates the terminal             16

17
     architecture at randomly chosen states and checks exact equality, not merely a small        17

18
     mean terminal error.                                                                        18

19
       The recursive Epstein–Zin domain is bV > 0. An exponential output ensures                 19

20
     positivity, while an interval transformation can keep bV between declared positive          20

21
     barriers. If bG > 0, one terminal-compatible positive representation is                     21

22                                                                                               22
                           bv(t, x ) = exp [log(bG ( x )) + ( T − t) N (t, x )] .
23                                                                                               23

24                                                                                               24
     Its terminal value is exact. It does not by itself impose a lateral exit payoff or a
25                                                                                               25
     two-sided barrier. These additional conditions must be built into the architecture or
26                                                                                               26
     measured explicitly. Clipping an invalid utility argument inside a fractional power
27                                                                                               27
     changes the equation and is not an acceptable hidden numerical repair.
28                                                                                               28

29                                                                                               29
                           11.2. From residual samples to a uniform bound
30                                                                                               30

31     Let r be K-Lipschitz on a domain in Rd . If every point is within distance h       V of   31

32   a validation point, the triangle inequality gives ∥r ∥∞ ≤ maxx∈V |r ( x )| + KhV . If K     32
     42

1    is estimated from samples rather than bounded, the resulting expression is itself a                      1

2    diagnostic, not a certified uniform bound.                                                               2

3      An integral bound needs more assumptions. Suppose the sampling density is at                           3

4    least p0 > 0, and each ball of radius r0 or less centered in the domain intersects the                   4

5    domain in volume at least c D r d . Let M = ∥r ∥∞ and assume M/(2K ) ≤ r0 . Near a                       5

6    point approaching the maximum, Lipschitz continuity gives |r | ≥ M/2 on a ball of                        6

7    radius M/(2K ). Hence                                                                                    7

8                                                                                   !1/(d+2)                  8
                                 p0 c D                              2d+2 K d Er2
9                   Er2 ≥        d +2       d
                                                M d +2 ,      M≤                               .              9
                             2          K                               p0 c D
10                                                                                                            10

11   For larger M, use the capped radius r0 and the corresponding two-case inequality. 11
12   A concentration argument is additionally needed to replace the population mean by                        12

13   an empirical one. This calculation explains why a dimension-free uniform certificate                     13

14   cannot be read directly from an average training loss.                                                   14

15     The action gap is treated similarly. If an action net has covering radius h A and the                  15

16   Hamiltonian is K A -Lipschitz in the action, the unobserved supremum exceeds the                         16

17   net maximum by at most K A h A . Thus the net gap plus this term is an upper bound. 17
18   A collection of successful deviations without a covering or duality argument is only                     18

19   a lower bound on exploitability.                                                                         19

18
       For the finite-horizon matching-bequest test at a > 0 > b, define Y = bV. The               18

19
     consumption domain is compact and bounded away from zero. At sufficiently small               19

20
     positive Y, c a Y −a/b < 1 uniformly in consumption, so f < 0. At sufficiently large          20

21
     Y, the reverse inequality holds and f > 0. Because dividing by negative b reverses            21

22
     the order of the utility endpoints, the large-Y constant is a lower utility barrier and       22

23
     the small-Y constant is an upper utility barrier. The matching bequest lies between           23

24
     them after enlarging the interval. Lipschitz scalar comparison then keeps each                24

25
     policy utility inside the interval, on which the aggregator and its utility derivative        25

26
     are bounded. This verifies the finite-horizon interpretation without claiming an              26

27
     unrestricted infinite-horizon result at those parameters.                                     27

                             13. ENDOGENOUS-PREFERENCE DETAILS
29                                                                                                 29
                        13.1. State restrictions and the reference experiment
30                                                                                                 30

31     In the baseline application, the risk-aversion interval lies strictly above one and         31

32   the process is reflected. The reference wealth problem is stopped at positive finite          32
     44

1    bounds. Consumption shares are bounded away from zero, adjustment efforts are                  1

2    bounded, and the horizon is finite. These choices bound the flow utility, its relevant         2

3    derivatives away from the terminal-boundary compatibility issue, and the bequest. 3
4    They provide a well-defined finite reference calculation. The numerical reflection             4

5    is a folded reflection of Brownian cubature endpoints; the wealth boundary is                  5

6    absorbing with the prescribed payoff. The same terminal and boundary values are                6

7    imposed for both adjustment-cost parameters.                                                   7

8      The grid reference operates in y = log X. Its drift is                                       8

9                                                                                                   9

10
                                by = r + p(µ − r ) − m − 12 p2 σX2 ,                                10

11                                                                                                  11
     and its covariance with u is ϱσu pσX . The two Brownian-factor columns can be
12                                                                                        12
     written (σu , ϱpσX )⊤ and (0, 1 − ϱ2 pσX )⊤ . Four symmetric cubature directions re-
                                  p
13                                                                                                  13
     produce this covariance. The correction − p2 σX2 /2 in log wealth is essential; omitting
14                                                                                                  14
     it would change the economic diffusion.
15                                                                                                  15
       For each action, the backward update is current utility times the time step plus
16                                                                                                  16
     the exponentially discounted positive-weight interpolation of next-period value.
17                                                                                                  17
     Feasible actions are enumerated on the declared 27-element grid. Because the
18                                                                                                  18
     terminal/exit payoff is fixed, raising k weakly lowers each action’s payoff and, by
19                                                                                                  19
     backward induction, lowers value at every state and time. The recorded arrays
20                                                                                                  20
     permit a direct check of this order. The reported grid-refinement difference is not an
21                                                                                                  21
     estimate of unrestricted neural approximation error.
22                                                                                                  22

23                                                                                                  23
                         13.2. Policy conditions and comparative statics
24                                                                                                  24

25     The consumption marginal utility in reference units is Uc       = c̄−1 (c/c̄)−u . Equating   25

26   it to VX yields c = c̄(c̄VX )−1/u . Adjustment maximizes θVu − kθ 2 /2 and hence               26

27   uses the projection of Vu /k under a box. Portfolio choice maximizes a quadratic               27

28   with coefficient X 2 σX2 VXX /2. The projected first-order solution is valid only when         28

29   this coefficient is negative. These conditions clarify why numerical curvature and             29

30   feasibility must be checked independently.                                                     30

31     The cross derivative VuX is an endogenous object. Its sign cannot be inferred from           31

32   the label “risk aversion” or from a negative shock correlation. Nor can the sign of            32
                                        NBO SUPPLEMENT                                      45

1    ∂k θ k be inferred by differentiating 1/k while keeping Vuk fixed. In contrast, the value    1

2    monotonicity in k holds policy by policy. At a differentiable optimum the envelope           2

3    formula follows because k appears only in the quadratic running cost. Changing               3

4    the normalization function κ (u) can change both adjustment and portfolio policies           4

5    even while leaving utility curvature in consumption unchanged.                               5

6      An observation model is needed for structural estimation. With latent u, uncer- 6
7    tainty in κ, and unknown adjustment or shock parameters, a sensitivity plot for              7

8    one policy component does not prove identification. A practical estimation exercise          8

9    would compute moment derivatives jointly, inspect their rank after normalization             9

10   restrictions, and propagate numerical approximation error into the implied moment            10

11   map. Those are distinct tasks from solving the controlled economy.                           11

                                 14. TEMPORAL-SELF DEVIATIONS
13                                                                                                13

14     For the two-exponential kernel, the component value under constant consump- 14
15   tion share m is Wj ( X ) = mb X b /[bλ j (m)], where λ j (m) = ρ j − b(r − m). A deviation   15

16   to share z for duration ϵ, followed by m, has component value at X = 1                       16

17                                     "                                     #                    17
                                     1 b 1 − e−λ j (z)ϵ                m  b
18                       Jj (z, ϵ) =     z              + e−λ j (z)ϵ           .                  18
                                     b      λ j (z)                  λ j (m)
19                                                                                                19

20   The continuous extension is used if λ j (z) = 0. The deviation gain is ∑ j w j Jj (z, ϵ) −   20

21   ∑ j w j Wj (1), with weights ( β, 1 − β). This is the quantity independently maximized 21
22   in the experiment, rather than a restatement of the equilibrium first-order condition. 22
23     At an equilibrium share, the first-order gain is nonpositive for every feasible            23

24   instantaneous share and equals zero at the equilibrium share. A finite-duration self         24

25   can generally choose a slightly different share, so the maximized gain need not be           25

26   exactly zero for positive duration. The required diagnostic is its gain divided by           26

27   duration as duration tends to zero. For β = 1 the ordinary exponential solution is           27

28   recovered.                                                                                   28

29     As κ grows, the kernel becomes increasingly concentrated near the present before           29

30   approaching its long-run component. Taking the instantaneous-deviation limit                 30

31   and taking κ → ∞ are conceptually different operations. The finite-κ model in this           31

32   paper explicitly specifies the order: the spike condition is derived for fixed κ, and        32
     46

1    any duration-limit comparative static is taken afterward. This avoids assigning an         1

2    undefined continuous-time meaning to a discrete present-bias multiplier.                   2

3                                                                                               3

4                       15. GAMES AND INDEPENDENT BEST RESPONSES                                4

5                                                                                               5
       A fixed rival profile turns each player’s problem into an ordinary controlled
6                                                                                               6
     Markov problem, so the main verification results apply player by player. The
7                                                                                               7
     derivative graph must implement this reduction. During player i’s actor step, every
8                                                                                               8
     rival policy and every derivative of player i’s critic is detached. During player i’s
9                                                                                               9
     critic step, all players’ actions are detached. A sum of these reporting losses can be
10                                                                                              10
     logged, but differentiation of a joint-profit objective is not equivalent to those block
11                                                                                              11
     steps.
12                                                                                              12
       The two-firm static diagnostic has best response qi = (1 − q j )/2 when interior. At
13                                                                                              13
     the joint-profit allocation (1/4, 1/4), the deviating firm chooses 3/8 and improves
14                                                                                              14
     profit from 1/8 to 9/64, a gain of 1/64. The own-action derivative is 1/4 at the
15                                                                                              15
     joint optimum. These numbers verify a graph-level distinction; they are not a
16                                                                                              16
     computation of the dynamic capital game.
17                                                                                              17
       For the dynamic problem, a reported equilibrium should include an indepen-
18                                                                                              18
     dent solution of each fixed-rival best-response problem, or an upper bound on
19                                                                                              19
     the unilateral Hamiltonian gap over the entire verification domain. A finite list of
20                                                                                              20
     unsuccessful deviations does not supply such an upper bound. Multiple initializa-
21                                                                                              21
     tions, asymmetric profiles, boundary actions, and the market-size normalization are
22                                                                                              22
     part of the same experiment. The specification in the main text retains the dynamic
23                                                                                              23
     Cournot application but does not invent a dynamic Nash table from the static
24                                                                                              24
     arithmetic.
25                                                                                              25

26                                                                                              26
                    16. STOCHASTIC HESSIAN OBJECTIVES AND COMPLEXITY
27                                                                                              27

28     Let B = Σ⊤ D2 vΣ, a symmetric matrix in Brownian-factor space. For Gaussian              28

29   probes, Isserlis’ identity gives E(z⊤ Bz) = tr B and Var(z⊤ Bz) = 2 tr( B2 ) = 2∥ B∥2F . 29
30   Averaging K independent probes divides the variance by K. Rademacher probes                30

31   instead have variance 2 ∑i̸= j Bij2 /K. The variance depends on the chosen covariance      31

32   factor and probe law, both of which must be specified.                                     32
                                        NBO SUPPLEMENT                                  47

1      Write qbj = q + ϵ j , with conditionally mean-zero independent errors. Then           1

2                                                                                            2
               E[(d − qb1 /2)(d − qb2 /2)] = (d − q/2)2 + 14 E[ϵ1 ϵ2 ] = (d − q/2)2 .
3                                                                                            3

4    Using one bank twice would retain its variance term. In the scalar Gaussian example,    4

5    a single-bank squared residual has expectation 1 − h + 3h2 /4, minimized at h = 2/3,    5

6    whereas the exact residual (1 − h/2)2 is minimized at h = 2. The independent            6

7    product restores the latter population objective. The saved Monte Carlo diagnostic      7

8    uses 400,000 pairs and records both empirical estimates; it does not claim that the     8

9    sampling error is identically zero.                                                     9

10     The core implementation uses exact Hessians to make the covariance contraction        10

11   independently testable. A Hessian-vector implementation can replace it without          11

12   changing the conditional-independence requirement. The cost of a Hessian-vector         12

13   product depends on the network graph, not just the state dimension. Total cost also     13

14   includes sampling, optimization, domain coverage, action maximization, and stop- 14
15   ping at an agreed error level. A quadratic-neural representation with a Lyapunov        15

16   solve has different approximation and optimization costs from a generic multi- 16
17   layer network. The inherited quadratic laboratory is explicitly the former; the new     17

18   dense nonlinear experiments actually train generic networks and Hessian-vector          18

19   estimators.                                                                             19

20                                                                                           20

21
                        17. REPRODUCTION AND FURTHER COMPARISONS                             21

22     From the repository root, execute the experiment program and then the regression      22

23   suite. The dependency versions and CPU setting are recorded with the results. The       23

24   experiment output distinguishes acceptance of a calibration or identity test from       24

25   execution of a reference computation. A separate reviewer-check output reproduces       25

26   the original twelve diagnostics against the archived original manuscript, not against   26

27   the revised article.                                                                    27

28     A broader method comparison should use one problem specification and evaluate         28

29   value error, policy error, boundary error, and the independent action gap at matched    29

30   accuracy. A direct neural-HJB comparator eliminates the actor with the same feasible    30

31   maximization routine. A temporal-regression comparator uses the same recursive          31

32   discretization and utility domain, without adding discount twice. A separability- 32
     48

1    aware classical comparator exploits all available economic structure. Network                 1

2    capacity, sampling, covariance treatment, random seeds, failures, wall time, and              2

3    memory should all be reported. R6 executes the architecture-matched direct-HJB                3

4    and finite-Bellman comparisons. The broader deep-BSDE comparison remains                      4

5    unexecuted and is not counted as a measured advantage. R7 neural-game evidence                5

6    and the additional R8 comparisons are reported in the integrated sections below.              6

7      The inherited read-only workflow is preserved. The separately scoped R6 replica- 7
8    tion workflow commits materialized source and raw recomputation outputs only                  8

9    to its designated candidate branch, without editing the review or earlier revision            9

10   branches. Hashes and exact source commits distinguish these artifacts. No workflow            10

11   rewrites a failed numerical accuracy target as a pass.                                        11

12                                                                                                 12
                        18. IMPLEMENTED ERROR ACCOUNTS AND ARITHMETIC
13                                                                                                 13

14     The implemented map returns a candidate and a verification record, rather than              14

15   assigning authority to an optimizer’s exit code. In the differential consumption              15

16   economy, the record identifies the binary64 weights, both stopping faces, all dyadic          16

17   leaves, global consumption maximization, and the error bounds for the continuous              17

18   diffusion. In NDU it instead identifies the finite state–action–time model, its positive      18

19   transition weights, and time-indexed evaluation and improvement errors. The                   19

20   dynamic-game record fixes the entire rival policy before an independent backward              20

21   best-response solve. An unchecked continuous-action or time-discretization term is            21

22   represented as unavailable in the machine-readable output, not as zero.                       22

23     For finite time levels, let δt = ∥vt − Tta vt+1 ∥∞ and ϵt = ∥ Tt vt+1 − Tta vt+1 ∥∞ . If    23

24   both maps are β t -Lipschitz, the triangle inequality gives                                   24

25                                                                                                 25
          ∥vt − Vta ∥ ≤ δt + β t ∥vt+1 − Vta+1 ∥,   ∥vt − Vt∗ ∥ ≤ δt + ϵt + β t ∥vt+1 − Vt∗+1 ∥.
26                                                                                                 26

27   Summing the two inequalities yields the regret account. The same recurrence with              27

28   a player’s unilateral gap bounds the gain from a dynamic deviation when that                  28

29   player’s policy evaluation is exact. Accounts must be checked at each time level:             29

30   a time-zero bound need not dominate a later-time bound when discounting and                   30

31   errors differ. The coarse-game regression exercises this distinction and verifies that        31

32   a missing pure equilibrium is reported rather than silently treated as exact.                 32
                                            NBO SUPPLEMENT                             49

1                           18.1. Full-domain interval implementation                        1

2
       An interval [l, u] encloses an exact real quantity. Each elementary operation ex- 2
3                                                                                            3
     pands its rounded endpoints with the adjacent binary64 number. Affine layers split
4                                                                                            4
     positive and negative weights and include a conservative dot-product forward-error
5                                                                                            5
     allowance. The arithmetic contract requires binary64 round-to-nearest arithmetic,
6
     the stated dot-product error bound, and absence of exceptional overflow or flush- 6
7                                                                                            7
     to-zero behavior. Stored parameters denote their exact binary64 real values; the
8                                                                                            8
     verifier does not assert an unrecorded decimal model identity.
9

     for the outward-enclosed range |z| < .334. The same expansion encloses log 2. Nei-
23                                                                                           23
     ther construction presumes correctly rounded library tanh or logarithm functions.
24                                                                                           24
     The saved test suite checks transcendental endpoints against eighty-digit arithmetic,
25                                                                                           25
     differentiates the trained network independently, and checks all certificate hashes
26                                                                                           26
     and leaf adjacencies. These tests supplement, but do not replace, the arithmetic
27                                                                                           27
     contract.
28                                                                                           28
       The scalar action objective h(c) = log c − pc has second derivative −1/c2 . If C
29                                                                                           29
     encloses both the actor and its maximizing alternative, strong concavity on their
30                                                                                           30
     joining segment gives
31                                                                                           31

32               h(c∗ ) − h(c) ≤ h′ (c)(c∗ − c) − (c∗ − c)2 /(2C2 ) ≤ C2 h′ (c)2 /2.         32
     50

1    The argument includes constrained endpoints. Taking the minimum with the natu- 1
2    ral interval action-gap enclosure markedly reduces verification cost, without using    2

3    a sampled gradient as a bound. Residual targets are decided on every leaf; reaching    3

4    a maximum subdivision depth does not change a failed target into a pass.               4

5                                                                                           5

6                                                                                           6
                              18.2. Application-specific hypotheses
7                                                                                           7

8      The stopped consumption diffusion is uniformly nondegenerate on its compact          8

9    domain; controls and coefficients are bounded and its endpoint data are continuous. 9
10   The smooth networks and strictly positive consumption map meet the regularity          10

11   and log-domain requirements of the verification argument. Both stopping-face           11

12   errors and the full action interval are checked. The upwind reference error is not a   12

13   premise of its continuous certificate.                                                 13

14     The NDU computation is a positive-weight, finite-action, finite-horizon economy      14

15   with prescribed discrete stopping and folded preference transitions. Positivity,       15

16   all terminal and wealth-face values, and every finite action are evaluated. The        16

17   continuous reflected PDE’s corner compatibility, continuous-action coverage error,     17

18   and diffusion approximation error are not inferred from these tests. Expanding a       18

19   feasible box changes the constrained economy; densifying a fixed box does not. The     19

20   sensitivity record keeps these operations separate.                                    20

21     The dense capital computation uses bounded nonlinear drift in log coordinates,       21

22   bounded positive consumption fractions, constant diffusion, and finite horizon. Its    22

23   flow has at most linear log-state growth and its terminal payoff at most quadratic     23

24   growth; these are integrable under the declared bounded-drift diffusion. The fitted    24

25   box is a sampling domain, not a state constraint. Its sampled differential residuals   25

26   do not check comparison barriers or uniform errors on Rd . Euler payoff estimates      26

27   carry both sampling error and unbounded-state/time-discretization issues not           27

28   covered by the reported Monte Carlo standard error.                                    28

29     The dynamic capital game is a fully enumerated finite approximation with             29

30   stopped joint boundaries. Stage equilibria and dynamic fixed-rival best responses      30

31   are numerical statements about that model. The continuous-control best-response        31

32   comparison assumptions in the main theorem remain logically distinct. The re- 32
                                       NBO SUPPLEMENT                                 51

1    tained homothetic recursive-utility and temporal-self examples use their stated        1

2    utility domains, bequests, and integrability conditions; R6 does not relabel them as   2

3    unrestricted neural experiments.                                                       3

4                                                                                           4
                          19. DESIGN, BUDGETS, AND REPRODUCIBILITY
5                                                                                           5

6      The stopped-consumption study uses float64 and the existing generic derivative       6

7    core. Identical seed labels fix initialization and collocation construction across     7

8    methods. L-BFGS iteration caps are matched, but line-search closure counts need        8

9    not match. Those counts, fit times, and separate verification times are saved. The     9

10   grid reference uses exact maximization within each upwind drift region, not the        10

11   learned actor. Reference comparisons omit four endpoint-adjacent nodes only for        11

12   finite-difference derivative comparisons; all endpoints enter the boundary check       12

13   and the interval certificate.                                                          13

14     The NDU actor predicts one of the finite feasible triples. Its normalized Bellman- 14
15   loss objective gives economically costly action mistakes more weight than ordinary     15

16   classification would. Absorbing boundary rows are excluded only from actor fitting,    16

17   because their actions have no effect; they remain in policy evaluation and verifi- 17
18   cation. The failed initial classification implementation is archived with its actual   18

19   outputs. Principal seed 11 misses the declared policy-regret target even after the     19

20   improvement; its result is not discarded. The direct finite-Bellman comparator uses    20

21   the same critic class and total inner-iteration cap.                                   21

22     The dense-capital primary study uses exact Hessians; the trace follow-up actu- 22
23   ally backpropagates through Hessian-vector products. Product and single-bank           23

24   implementations use four probes in total, but only the product splits them into        24

25   conditionally independent banks. The Monte Carlo regression separately checks          25

26   the value and gradient of the product objective with tolerances .012 and .025. An      26

27   unbiased objective can have higher variance, and no claim of superior finite-sample    27

28   accuracy follows merely from its expectation. The covariance in the retained experi- 28
29   ment is modest; the reported results reflect that scale.                               29

30     The initial high-dimensional state box was too narrow to certify the realized        30

31   policy paths. The wider-box design is therefore identified as a follow-up, not a       31

32   preregistered experiment. Forty- and eighty-step policy simulations share the same     32
     52

1    seed convention across methods at a given resolution. They are not a coupled           1

2    Brownian extrapolation across resolutions. Raw per-path payoffs allow paired           2

3    policy comparisons, but neither a Monte Carlo confidence interval nor a critic– 3
4    payoff agreement proves closeness to the optimal value.                                4

5      Each executed program records all declared seeds, its budget, residual or devia- 5
6    tion arrays, and an explicit scope. Process peak resident memory is a high-water       6

7    mark of the process, potentially including earlier runs. It is not an isolated memory- 7
8    complexity measurement for an individual method. CPU timings are execution- 8
9    specific and do not reproduce the historical accelerator claims. The independent       9

10   raw-output workflow binds its source commit and file hashes; the source archive        10

11   keeps the R2 manuscript and supplement byte-for-byte for comparison.                   11

7      These estimates use 2,048 paths and 80 Euler steps from zero log capital. Forty- 7
