12                                                                                             12
          21. PROOF AND IMPLEMENTATION OF THE CONTINUOUS-ACTION ENVELOPE
13                                                                                             13

14     We use the notation of the main text’s budget-safe envelope proposition. The            14

15   nodal economy is fixed before verification. Its terminal and stopping payoffs, prim- 15
16   itive coefficients, interpolation abscissae, and compact action box are part of the       16

17   object being bounded. This is stronger than checking many action samples and              17

18   weaker than verifying the continuous-state diffusion without a discretization ac- 18
19   count.                                                                                    19

20                                                                                             20

21
                          21.1. Complete-cover invariant and backward proof                    21

22     At a given state and time, the initial action box covers the entire feasible set. Bisec- 22
23   tion replaces one closed box by two closed boxes with the same union. Their shared        23

24   face is harmless. Induction on the number of subdivisions preserves coverage. A           24

25   pruned box is retained logically through the inequality u B ≤ ci + τ, where u B is        25

26   an outward upper enclosure on that whole box, and ci is a lower enclosure at a            26

27   feasible incumbent. If the incumbent later increases, the previous pruning inequality     27

28   remains true. Consequently every feasible action is either in an unresolved box or        28

29   in a pruned box with payoff at most the current threshold. Taking the maximum of          29

30   the threshold and all unresolved upper bounds dominates the action supremum,              30

31   whether or not the supremum is attained. Termination on a numerical budget does           31

32   not change this argument.                                                                 32
                                         NBO SUPPLEMENT                                   57

1      At N, the terminal inequalities are given. If Vn∗+1 ≤ Un+1 , positivity gives           1

2    Tn Vn∗+1 ≤ Tn Un+1 ≤ Un . If Ln+1 ≤ Vnπ+1 , the same argument gives Ln ≤ Tnπn Ln+1 ≤ 2
3    Tnπn Vnπ+1 = Vnπ . Feasibility implies Vnπ ≤ Vn∗ . This proves the main envelope inequal- 3
4    ity. Substochasticity further gives                                                       4

5                                                                                              5
                                 ∥ Tn w − Tn z∥∞ ≤ qn ∥w − z∥∞ .
6                                                                                              6

7    Applying this inequality to Un − Tn Vn∗+1 yields the stated excess recursion and its      7

8    product-sum expansion. The proof uses qn > 0, not a stationary contraction assump- 8
9    tion. Outward addition is used for the pruning threshold and final subtraction. The       9

10   implementation records a requested tolerance and a retained optimization bracket          10

11   separately. A budget-exhausted bracket is never replaced by that tolerance. Q.E.D.        11

12                                                                                             12

13                   21.2. Interpolation, reflection, stopping, and arithmetic                 13

14                                                                                             14
       For a cell with coordinates (u0 , u1 ) × (y0 , y1 ), the continuation is
15                                                                                             15

16            Iw(u, y) = (1 − s)(1 − t)w00 + s(1 − t)w10 + (1 − s)tw01 + stw11 ,               16

17                                      u − u0           y − y0                                17
                                   s=           ,   t=           .
18
                                        u1 − u0          y1 − y0                               18

19   For fixed u, this is affine in y, so its extremes on a subrectangle occur at the two y    19

20   endpoints. At either endpoint it is affine in u, so the extremes occur at the two u end- 20
21   points. Applying this argument to every intersected cell proves the corner enclosure. 21
22   Nonnegative interpolation weights also permit interval nodal inputs: evaluating the       22

23   corner expressions outward encloses every admissible nodal realization. A separate        23

24   valid enclosure evaluates the rectangle center and adds cell-slope bounds over every      24

25   intersected cell. Slopes are computed from stored nodal differences, not estimated        25

26   from random derivative samples.                                                           26

27     Preference reflection is piecewise affine. Its extrema on an interval occur at the      27

28   interval endpoints or a crossed folding point. The allowed one-step movement is           28

29   checked to exclude unhandled multiple folds. For log wealth, boxes fully inside           29

30   the domain use interpolation, boxes fully outside use the prescribed clipped exit         30

31   payoff, and boxes crossing an exit face use the union of both. On the stored grid the     31

32   boundary nodes equal the exact prescribed payoff; at an off-grid exit the analytic        32
     58

1    payoff need not equal its nodal interpolant. The union construction avoids an              1

2    unjustified global smoothness or Lipschitz assumption across that switch.                  2

3      The implementation inherits the archived outward elementary and transcen- 3
4    dental routines. Products, quotients, powers through logarithm and exponential,            4

5    and endpoint rounding are enclosed. The logarithm’s input is bounded below by              5

6    .02; the preference denominator is separated from zero because u ∈ [1.2, 3]. The           6

7    recorded control model treats the stored binary64 primitive coefficients and ab- 7
8    scissae as exact real inputs. The evidence also tests agreement with the inherited         8

9    training evaluator on the finite action grid. Such a regression check is useful for        9

10   implementation consistency but is not the proof of global action coverage. That            10

11   proof is the maintained union invariant and the interval inclusion property. The           11

12   arithmetic account is conditional on the specified floating-point execution model,         12

13   not a formal verification of hardware, libraries, or the Python interpreter.               13

14                                                                                              14

15                                                                                              15
                      21.3. A common upper envelope for multiple policies
16                                                                                              16
       The optimal upper array depends on the economy, not on which feasible incum-
17                                                                                              17
     bent produced the lower search values. Thus the completed upper array can be
18                                                                                              18
     reused for any frozen feasible policy on the same state, time, and continuous-action
19                                                                                              19
     domains. We separately propagate each policy’s lower and upper payoff arrays and
20                                                                                              20
     subtract its lower array from the common optimal upper array. Costs are reported
21                                                                                              21
     both as shared verification and as the cost of an individual policy evaluation. Shar-
22                                                                                              22
     ing a verifier does not make its cost vanish. It also does not justify transferring the
23                                                                                              23
     upper array to a changed wealth box, action constraint, utility normalization, time
24                                                                                              24
     step, or interpolation rule.
25                                                                                              25

26                                                                                              26
               22. SEPARATING NUMERICAL AND CONTINUOUS-ECONOMY ERROR
27                                                                                              27

28     The following result specifies the transfer work needed in addition to a nodal cer- 28
29   tificate. It is not used to set an uncomputed transfer term to zero. Let Tn be the exact   29

30   economic one-step optimality operator with its continuously monitored stopping             30

31   and reflection rule, and let Tnπ be the operator for a declared continuous-state im- 31
32   plementation of the numerical policy. Let In be a positive, sup-norm-nonexpansive          32
                                         NBO SUPPLEMENT                                       59

1    interpolation from grid values to the economic state domain. Suppose the exact                1

2    operators are qn -Lipschitz on the comparison class.                                          2

3                                                                                                  3
       P ROPOSITION 1—Transfer with separate optimal and policy defects: Suppose
4                                                                                                  4
     terminal interpolation error is at most B and, along the relevant nodal continuations,
5                                                                                                  5

6
                                 ∥Tn In+1 v∗n+1 − In Tn v∗n+1 ∥∞ ≤ d∗n ,                           6

7                                                                                                  7
                               ∥Tnπ In+1 vπn+1 − In Tnπn vπn+1 ∥∞ ≤ dπn .
8                                                                                                  8
                                    j     j        j
9    Define D ∗N = D π
                     N = B and Dn = dn + qn Dn+1 for j ∈ {∗, π }. Then the exact economic
                                                                                                   9

10   values satisfy                                                                                10

11                                                                                                 11
                            0 ≤ Vn∗ − Vnπ ≤ In (Un − Ln ) + Dn∗ + Dnπ .
12                                                                                                 12
                                                ∗
13 The action-domain approximation belongs in d n , while policy implementation, boundary          13

14   monitoring, state interpolation, and time/cubature errors may affect both defects.            14

15                                                                                                 15
       P ROOF : Insert Tn In+1 v∗n+1 between the exact and interpolated nodal Bellman
16                                                                                                 16
     values. The Lipschitz property bounds the propagated value difference by qn Dn∗+1 ,
17                                                                                                 17
     and the displayed defect bounds the remaining difference by d∗n . Backward induc-
18                                                                                                 18
     tion gives ∥Vn∗ − In v∗n ∥ ≤ Dn∗ . The same argument gives the fixed-policy bound.
19                                                                                                 19
     Subtract the two decompositions and use positivity of In and v∗n − vπ
                                                                         n ≤ Un − Ln .
20                                                                                                 20
     Feasibility gives the lower inequality.                                              Q.E.D.
21                                                                                                 21

22     The proposition can use local regularity, coupling, barrier, or weak-approximation 22
23   estimates to bound its defects; it does not claim that those hypotheses hold au- 23
24   tomatically for a fitted critic near a stopped boundary. In the present two-state 24
25   computations, action coverage is operationally bounded on the nodal economy. 25
26   The continuous-state/time defects remain explicitly uncomputed. Nested grids 26
27
     and common frozen policies diagnose sensitivity but do not provide the one-sided 27
     inequalities required by this proposition.

                     23. STRATEGIC DEVIATIONS AND MISSING PURE STAGES
27                                                                                              27

28     For a fixed Markov rival profile in a finite-horizon finite-state/action game, condi- 28
29   tion on any history. The own player’s conditional payoff from a randomized current         29

30   action is a convex combination of pure-action payoffs with the same continuation           30

31   best-response values. It cannot exceed the largest such payoff. Backward induction         31

32   therefore bounds every randomized and history-dependent unilateral policy by the           32
                                             NBO SUPPLEMENT                                               61

1    Markov pure-action best-response recursion. Outward propagation then provides                             1

2    a bound over this full deviation class, not merely the particular local deviations                        2

3    observed during actor training.                                                                           3

4      Existence of a pure equilibrium in a fitted one-step game is a different matter. 4
5    If none exists, minimum-defect selection produces a feasible profile, not an exact                        5

6    equilibrium. The independent dynamic best response still evaluates that profile                           6

7    correctly. The following records give every missing pure stage in the six principal                       7

8    R7 neural runs, including time index, state, and minimum maximum stage defect. 8
9    Indices begin at zero. The six runs have 6 × 30 × 225 = 40,500 active state-time                          9

10   nodes; the five recorded exceptions are not excluded from their final dynamic                             10

11   bounds. The profit targets are absolute units of the displayed per-firm payoff. Raw                       11

                  25. HISTORICAL NUMERICAL RECORD AND SOURCE PRESERVATION                                     29

30     The preceding manuscript and supplement are archived byte-for-byte under                               30

31   the R8 revision directory. Every earlier economic equation, application, numerical                       31

32   source, raw array, failed run, and referee report remains in the repository history                      32
                                        NBO SUPPLEMENT                                      65

1    and the candidate tree. The main text now presents the successful R7 finite-policy          1

2    computations and the new continuous-action study at the point where the corre- 2
3    sponding economic model is introduced. Earlier R6 policy failures and non-neural            3

4    game results are not relabelled as current neural results. Their original discussions       4

5    are reproduced below as dated historical material.                                          5

6                                                                                                6

7                        25.1. R6 preference-policy discussion (historical)                      7

8                                                                                                8
                       25.2. Neural solution and action-domain sensitivity
9                                                                                                9

10     We now apply multilayer NBO to the same two-state economy, with the original              10

11   cardinal utility, correlation, reflection rule, and stopping payoff unchanged. At each      11

12   backward time level a two-hidden-layer, width-thirty-two tanh critic approximates           12

13   its own Bellman continuation; a multilayer categorical actor selects among feasible         13

14   actions. The reference solution is computed separately and is not used as a training        14

15   target. The actor is fitted before policy evaluation at that time level, with the next- 15
16   period critic fixed. The direct comparator eliminates the actor by enumeration of           16

17   the same action set. Both methods have a cap of 160 L-BFGS inner iterations per             17

18   time level, divided 100/60 between critic and actor for NBO.                                18

19     An initial unweighted classification implementation produced a large policy               19

20   loss. Absorbing wealth-face labels, for which every action has identical value,             20

21   contaminated the policy fit; classification error also ignored the economic cost of         21

22   a mistaken low-consumption action. The retained implementation excludes those               22

23   faces from actor training and minimizes expected normalized Bellman action loss,            23

24   with a small cross-entropy term. The pilot source, weights, and failed output are           24

25   preserved. This change repairs an implementation failure rather than redefining the         25

26   economic objective: the deployed action is still evaluated against all finite feasible      26

27   alternatives.                                                                               27

28     For the principal comparison, (nu , n x , Nt ) = (17, 25, 20) and k = 1. The ex- 28
29   panded action set contains 175 triples, with c/X ∈ {.02, .05, .10, .20, .30}, p ∈           29

30   {0, .375, .75, 1.125, 1.5}, and θ ∈ {−.45, −.30, −.15, 0, .15, .30, .45}. Every time–state– 30
31   action combination enters the error accounts in the main-text Bellman error accounts. 31
32   Independently solving the fixed learned policy gives its actual regret relative to the      32
     66

14                                                                                                                 14

15                                                                                                                 15

16   finite reference. Midpoint value errors and derivative comparisons are additional                             16

17   diagnostics, not continuous-domain certificates. The three-seed record contains a                             17

18   failed NBO accuracy target and reports it without exclusion. The direct comparator                            18

19   is more accurate at this budget. A categorical actor therefore has a measurable                               19

20   approximation cost even when the critic class is identical. Neural representation                             20

21   alone is not a reason to replace an inexpensive two-state grid solver; the grid here                          21

22   supplies an unusually complete audit of the neural computation.                                               22

23      Action bounds are part of the economy, not innocuous numerical constants. The                              23

24   sensitivity study reproduces the referee’s action-set comparison and extends it to                            24

25   1,053 actions in the same expanded box, a wider consumption–portfolio–adjustment                              25

26   box, a wider wealth domain, and two finer space–time grids, for both k = 1 and                                26

27   k = 5. The table in the supplement records lower- and upper-bound frequencies for                             27

28   every control. Consumption and portfolio bounds remain frequently active after                                28

29   the first expansion. Thus state-grid convergence of the old 27-action model does                              29

30   not establish convergence to an unconstrained control optimum. The wider sets                                 30

31   describe different constrained economies; refinement within a fixed box is a different                        31

32   comparison. No continuous-action coverage or controlled-diffusion discretization                              32

16                                                                                                                        16
                                25.3. R6 dynamic-game discussion (historical)
17                                                                                                                        17
                                  25.4. A dynamic capital-game computation
18                                                                                                                        18

19      For two firms, we compute the finite-horizon capital game above with A = 1,                                       19

20   P0 = 2, δ = .1, σK = .15, ρ = .04, T = 3, investment in [0, .8], and capital in [.05, 1.5]2 . 20
21   Terminal or joint-domain exit pays .5Ki . The baseline quadratic adjustment co- 21
22   efficients are b1 = b2 = 1; an asymmetric experiment uses b2 = 1.2. Independent                                      22

23   diffusion cubature and positive interpolation define the finite dynamic game. In- 23
24   vestment nets include both bounds.                                                                                   24

25      Backward induction constructs the two continuation-payoff matrices at every                                       25

26   state and time. The solver enumerates pure stage equilibria; alternative deterministic                               26

27   tie rules are retained. If no pure equilibrium exists, the solver records this fact and                              27

28   the smallest maximum stage deviation it finds, rather than declaring equilibrium. 28
29   After fixing the entire computed rival policy, a separate backward optimization permits                              29

30   every own action at every state and time. Its value minus the profile payoff is                                      30

31   the unilateral exploitability map. A one-period check with a frozen continuation                                     31

32   value alone would not perform this test. All six principal finite-game cases have                                    32
     68

1    pure equilibria at every stage and zero detected exploitability to the reported           1

2    arithmetic precision. A deliberately coarse regression case has a missing pure stage      2

3    equilibrium and is reported as approximate, with time-indexed deviation bounds. 3
4    The table includes fixed-market M2 = 1 and growing-market M2 = 2 normalizations           4

5    as distinct economies; the resulting investment differences do not prove an N → ∞         5

6    limit. Binding-investment frequencies, alternative equilibrium selections, and the        6

7    asymmetric case are in the raw record. This is an actual dynamic best-response            7

8    computation, not the static 1/64 diagnostic. It is a finite-grid game result; a trained   8

9    neural game and a continuous-state equilibrium bound are different objects and are        9

10   not inferred from it.                                                                     10

1                  27. FRESH TRAINING, CORRECTION STORAGE, AND TOTAL COST                                    1

2                                                                                                            2
       The new spatial refinement is genuinely nested: every coarse spatial node is a
3                                                                                                            3
     fine spatial node, and every coarse date is a fine date. A trained fine-grid policy is
4                                                                                                            4
     not substituted into the frozen-policy historical experiment. The guard acts only on
5                                                                                                            5
     the chosen policy’s evaluation target. Its storage and query cost are explicit, and the
6                                                                                                            6
     actor-free comparison receives the same correction.
7                                                                                                            7

1                  28. AN ABSOLUTE BENCHMARK AND PAIRED CAPITAL PAYOFFS                                      1

2
       The continuous analytical bound in the article applies to the original unbounded- 2
3                                                                                                            3
     state capital economy from the specified initial state. The simulations below assess
4                                                                                                            4
     an explicit feasible schedule on the same discretized Brownian paths as the historical
5                                                                                                            5
     learned policies. A narrow Monte Carlo interval cannot remove unknown Euler
6                                                                                                            6
     bias. The analytical bound and the simulation comparison have different, stated
7                                                                                                            7
     roles.
8                                                                                                            8

9
                                                 TABLE XXIX                                                  9

21                                                                                                           21
                           29. CONTINUOUS-CAPITAL REPLICATION DETAILS
22                                                                                                           22
       The new study uses the identical capital flow, terminal payoff, diffusion covari-
23                                                                                                           23
     ance, primitive action interval, and dense coupling rule as the historical experiment.
24                                                                                                           24
     A regression test compares the numerical functions and matrices directly. Ten and
25                                                                                                           25
     twenty dimensions reuse the existing economies; fifty dimensions extends their
26                                                                                                           26
     declared generator. There are d + 1 independent Brownian drivers and a full-rank
27                                                                                                           27
     d-dimensional state covariance. These are different counts.
28                                                                                                           28

29                                                                                                           29
                                   29.1. Complete fitted-policy record

6    RMS and gap are diagnostics on 128 held-out states, not uniform certificates. Peak RSS is the separate   6

7    process high-water mark, including the interpreter and libraries. Every requested fit is retained. All   7

8    raw optimizer histories, negative trace-product batches, saved weights, sample counts, and bisection     8

9    counts are committed.   The output JSON records negative trace-product batches, all                      9

10   checkpoint losses and elapsed times, state-query and bisection counts, raw and                           10

11   guarded deployment costs, weight hashes, and failures. The initial output layer is                       11

12   zero, so the actor initially equals the common feasible schedule. Its guarantee is                       12

13   structural even before training. The protocol was developed after local pilots and is                    13

14   a pinned reproduction design, not a preregistered confirmatory experiment.                               14

23                                                                                                          23

24                                                                                                          24
     Quadrature endpoints are outward, not an error tolerance passed to an ordinary numerical integrator.
25                                                                                                          25
     Zero radius denotes the exact feasible schedule. Printed upper endpoints are rounded upward. The
26                                                                                                          26
     same matrix majorant is used for each dimension.
27                                                                                                          27

28                                                                                                          28
                               29.3. Deployment and arithmetic contract
29                                                                                                          29

30     The interval schedule guard uses the inherited binary64 arithmetic implementa- 30
31   tion with outward elementary operations and polynomial transcendental bounds. 31
32   The additional square-root routine verifies squared endpoints and handles a zero                       32
                                         NBO SUPPLEMENT                                  81

1    lower endpoint explicitly. An SVD proposes the spectral majorant, but the interval        1

2    factorization must verify it. A bad matrix shape, nonfinite matrix, nonpositive pivot,    2

3    mismatched matrix hash, empty inward tube, or out-of-horizon clock produces               3

4    an error. There is no fall-through from a failed arithmetic check to an empirical         4

5    maximum.                                                                                  5

6      The actor’s neural proposal need not be evaluated by validated transcendental           6

7    routines to maintain its action constraint: its finite output is projected onto the       7

8    inward interval. The theoretical result concerns the resulting continuous-time feed- 8
9    back rule. Euler paths approximate its payoff, and their bias remains uncomputed. A       9

10   sampled Euler path is not claimed to be an exact draw of the continuous controlled        10

11   diffusion. A finite proposal failure is recorded separately from the mathematical         11

12   fact that the specified fallback remains in the tube.                                     12

13                                                                                             13

14                                                                                             14

15
                             29.4. Paired path archive and interpretation                      15

16
       For each dimension, a fixed generator with seed 20261004 + d produces the 160- 16
17                                                                                             17
     step Brownian increments. Summing consecutive increments produces the coarser
18                                                                                             18
     40- and 80-step paths. The same increments are used for the schedule, all three actors,
19                                                                                             19
     and all six direct fits. The archive contains the increments themselves, every payoff
20                                                                                             20
     vector, and all finest-level terminal states. All reported mean and interval endpoints
21                                                                                             21
     are recomputed from these arrays by the audit. Seed-averaged comparisons first
22                                                                                             22
     average the three fixed seed payoffs within a path; the confidence interval then
23                                                                                             23
     concerns independent paths, not independent training seeds. No sampling interval
24                                                                                             24
     is subtracted from a continuous upper bound to obtain an unsupported policy
25                                                                                             25
     certificate.
26                                                                                             26

                           30.1. Executed calibration and interpretation
23                                                                                           23

24     The replication program trains a homothetic neural subfamily: a positive value        24

25   coefficient, a sigmoid consumption-share output, and a projected portfolio-share        25

26   output. These are neural output parameters in a known invariant representation,         26

27   not an unrestricted multilayer approximation. The two Adam optimizers have              27

28   learning rates .025 and .008; each outer iteration contains three critic updates and    28

29   one actor update. There are 1,600 outer iterations and seeds 0, 1, 2. All attempted     29

30   runs, including their coefficient and policy traces, are saved. These calibrations      30

31   optimize the stationary coefficient equation and then check the resulting coefficient   31

32   against the matching bequest; the coefficient error is a boundary error in that         32

13                                                                                                     13
                 31.1. A well-defined validation problem at the retained parameters
14                                                                                                     14

15     The numerical calibration uses (6) as a finite-horizon matching-bequest test, not as            15

16   an unqualified infinite-horizon theorem. Choose T > 0, stop wealth on leaving [ x, x̄ ]           16

17   with 0 < x < x̄ < ∞, and impose the candidate Kx b /b on the stopping boundary                    17

18   and at T. Consumption shares are in a compact positive interval containing .0455,                 18

19   and portfolio shares are in a compact interval containing .3.                                     19

20     For these retained parameters, a > 0 and b < 0. Since c ranges over a compact                   20

21   positive interval, there exist 0 < Y < Y < ∞ such that f (c, Y/b) ≤ 0 and f (c, Y/b) ≥            21

22   0 for every feasible c. Indeed, c a Y −a/b tends to zero as Y ↓ 0 and to infinity as Y ↑ ∞.       22

23   Enlarge the interval so that it contains the boundary candidate. The constants Y/b                23

24   and Y/b are respectively lower and upper barriers for the backward utility equation. 24
25   Scalar BSDE comparison therefore keeps every policy utility in this interval. The                 25

26   aggregator is Lipschitz there. The smooth homothetic candidate maximizes the                      26

27   Hamiltonian and matches all stopping payoffs; Theorem 2 identifies it as the value                27

28   of this specified finite-horizon problem.                                                         28

29     The choice of a matching bequest is an analytical validation device. It does                    29

30   not establish that the same candidate is selected by every infinite-horizon recur- 30
31   sive formulation. A separate infinite-horizon application must check its utility- 31
32   process class and transversality, particularly when risk aversion and intertemporal- 32
                                        NBO SUPPLEMENT                                    85

1    complementarity parameters straddle one (Herdegen et al., 2021). The paper retains         1

2    that economic application, but does not use a scalar root to bypass its selection          2

3    issue.                                                                                     3

4      A critic can enforce bV > 0 through bvξ = exp( Nξ ). With a positive terminal            4

5    target, logarithmic interpolation between bG and the neural output enforces both           5

6    the terminal condition and the power domain. Additional barriers or bounded                6

7    transformations enforce a declared invariant interval. Such transformations are            7

8    stated in the architecture, not imposed retrospectively after a failed evaluation.         8

9                                                                                               9

10                              31.2. Stability and baseline design                             10

11                                                                                              11
       The recursive experiment in Table XXXIII tests the corrected sign, coefficient,
12                                                                                              12
     and block graph. It does not compare independent DDPG training trajectories.
13                                                                                              13
     The reviewed manuscript’s constructed oscillating curve and manually entered
14                                                                                              14
     coordinates remain in the archival source, but have no evidentiary role in this
15                                                                                              15
     revision. A matched neural comparison must use the same utility process, domain,
16                                                                                              16
     bequest, action restrictions, training budget, and held-out evaluation quantities. It
17                                                                                              17
     must report all failed seeds and value-domain violations. Neither a zero-centered
18                                                                                              18
     target nor three successful homothetic calibrations establishes general relative
19                                                                                              19
     stability.

1                      32.3. Independent two-state reference and identification                          1

2                                                                                                        2

3                                                                                                        3

4                                                                                                        4

5      The executable reference solves the finite-horizon problem with T = 1, u ∈ [1.2, 3],              5

6    wealth stopped at [.5, 2], and G (u, X ) = X 1−u /(1 − u) at exit and terminal time. Pa-            6

7    rameters are (r, µ, σX , σu , ϱ, ρ) = (.02, .08, .2, .08, −.3, .04). The finite action set is the   7

8    product of c/X ∈ {.02, .05, .1}, p ∈ {0, .375, .75}, and θ ∈ {−.15, 0, .15}. A four-point           8

9    Brownian cubature with positive interpolation weights performs backward induc- 9
10   tion. The wealth boundary is imposed at every time step; preference overshoots                      10

11   are reflected. This reference defines a finite-action numerical approximation, not                  11

12   continuous-action optimality.                                                                       12

13     At (u, X ) = (2.1, 1), the original 17 × 25 state grid and 20 time steps give values              13

14   −11.915211 and −11.956983 for k = 1 and k = 5. The 25 × 37 grid and 40 time                         14

15   steps give −11.866768 and −11.908738. Both select (c/X, p, θ ) = (.1, .75, −.15) at                 15

16   that point. The approximately .048 refinement change is reported separately from                    16

17   training error. The higher-cost value is weakly lower at every recorded node and                    17

18   time. These original references remain useful consistency checks; Section 32.4 sup- 18
19   plies actual neural fits and shows how strongly the finite action domain affects their              19

20   economic comparison.                                                                                20

21     The model also specifies what a structural estimation exercise would require. For                 21

22   observations Yj = M(St j , at j ; ϑ ) + ε j , let m(ϑ ) denote selected population moments          22

23   of the induced observation process. Local identification requires a rank condition                  23

24   on Dϑ m, after accounting for latent preference states, normalization parameters,                   24

25   and observation noise. Variation of a simulated policy with k alone is insufficient. 25
26   Numerical value and policy errors must additionally be propagated into moment                       26

27   errors. The endogenous-preference application is therefore retained as an economic                  27

28   model with an explicit internal margin, not used as evidence that its structural                    28

29   parameters have already been identified from data.                                                  29

30     The next comparison is the retained R8 evidence under its original enclosure. 30
31   The new signed envelope and fresh nested-grid policies are reported separately in                   31

32   Section 37; historical failure counts are not overwritten.                                          32
                                        NBO SUPPLEMENT                                    89

1                32.4. Neural preference policies and the role of the action oracle            1

2                                                                                              2
       We compute the original preference-adjustment economy with the cardinal utility,
3                                                                                              3
     shock correlation, reflected preference state, and wealth stopping payoff specified
4                                                                                              4
     above. The main grid has 17 preference points, 25 log-wealth points, and 20 time
5                                                                                              5
     steps over a unit horizon. Adjustment cost is k = 1. The expanded control box is
6                                                                                              6

7                  m = c/X ∈ [.02, .30],       p ∈ [0, 1.5],    θ ∈ [−.45, .45].               7

8                                                                                              8
     The inherited finite-action comparison uses the 175 triples formed by five con-
9                                                                                              9
     sumption shares, five portfolio shares, and seven adjustment efforts, including all
10                                                                                             10
     endpoints. This is the same constrained economy across the matched finite methods,
11                                                                                             11
     not a change of utility normalization.
12                                                                                             12
       Two experiments distinguish action representation from action optimization.
13                                                                                             13
     First, the R7 finite study trains two-hidden-layer, width-32 tanh critics and cate-
14                                                                                             14
     gorical actors. At each backward step it computes the complete 175-action payoff
15                                                                                             15
     vector using the current continuation critic. Its exact argmax provides a label, and
16                                                                                             16
     the vector also supplies the actor’s expected action-loss objective. Thus the train-
17                                                                                             17
     ing oracle is exhaustive, even when the final actor is accurate. It is a useful neural
18                                                                                             18
     policy-representation experiment, but not evidence that the actor avoided Bellman
19                                                                                             19
     maximization.
20                                                                                             20
       Second, R8 trains a continuous-output actor directly through its own positive-
21                                                                                             21
     weight payoff. It never receives an all-action value vector or an exact argmax label.
22                                                                                             22
     The actor receives 160 Adam updates per time level; the continuation critic receives
23                                                                                             23
     320 Adam updates and up to 300 L-BFGS iterations, with failed polishing steps
24                                                                                             24
     rolled back. The actor-free direct comparator optimizes eight independent bounded
25                                                                                             25
     controls at every state for the same 160 iterations, followed by the same critic fit.
26                                                                                             26
     Every restart and rejected action query is charged. Neither procedure invokes a Ric-
27                                                                                             27
     cati solution, an analytically invariant policy class, or the independently computed
28                                                                                             28
     reference during training.
29                                                                                             29

30     Finite-action neural policies. The raw R7 actor is the primary neural representation    30

31   result. Its full dynamic policy loss is below the declared .1 target for each fixed seed. 31
32   A top-four shortlist and a separately reported correction map further reduce the          32
     90

1    loss. The correction map is not part of the raw network. The complete finite policy,                       1

2    not only one-step deviations against a fitted critic, is evaluated by independent                          2

3    backward recursion. These results repair the earlier R6 actor failures, which remain                       3

20                                                                                                              20

24     Continuous-action optimization without exact labels. The initial continuous actor                        24

25   and direct-gradient pilot fail the .1 deviation diagnostic. The actor can saturate                         25

26   at a portfolio bound while a lower portfolio share improves continuation value. 26
27   Four actor heads with output reinitialization do not cure that failure in the retained                     27

28   pilot. We therefore execute a disclosed local-improvement variant: compare the                             28

29   actor proposal with eight box corners, then perform two coordinate-search sweeps                           29

30   at each of eight decreasing step sizes. The same local search is applied to the                            30

31   direct comparator. An actor-free ablation omits all gradient action updates and                            31

32   starts the identical search from feasible proposals. This ablation measures how                            32
                                              NBO SUPPLEMENT                                                91

20   three quantities. The raw-network loss against the finite reference tests the actor                         20

21   alone. The augmented finite-deviation gain permits all 175 inherited actions and                            21

22   the learned continuous action at every state and time; it is a lower bound on missed                        22

23   continuous-action gains. The complete action-box upper bound in Proposition 1                               23

24   is a separate verification result. A small augmented gain is not inserted into that                         24

25   proposition as if it were a uniform upper bound. Local changes occur at nearly                              25

26   every active state-time node in the principal hybrid runs, so this experiment does                          26

27   not describe a sparse correction map or an actor-only controller. The action cover                          27

20     Alternative representations and economic units. Additional same-model compar- 20
21   isons use a gated neural continuation architecture and tensor Chebyshev projection. 21
22   The gated method uses DGM-style state-dependent gates (Sirignano and Spiliopou- 22
23   los, 2018), but here it is fitted to finite Bellman targets, not called a mesh-free                    23

24   differential DGM solver. The projection exploits the same two-state geometry and                       24

25   imposes the same wealth-boundary lifting. Degrees, parameter counts, all-action                        25

26   costs, failures, and actual accuracy appear in the supplement. These are executed                      26

27   post-review comparisons, not results attributed to the earlier unexecuted develop- 27
28   ment protocol. A generic semilinear BSDE comparator is not substituted for this                        28

29   controlled-covariance, stopped problem without reformulating its second-order                          29

30   control term.                                                                                          30

1    The updated parameter is ϕi , not the full vector of all players’ parameters. Rival          1

2    actions are detached, and player j’s loss never contributes to player i’s actor gradient. 2
3    An explicit regression test exercises this graph.                                            3

4      For each player, define the unilateral Hamiltonian gap by maximizing Hi over               4

5    ai while keeping rivals fixed. Applying Theorem 2 to that best-response prob- 5
6    lem bounds its finite-horizon exploitability by 2e Li T Bi + A Li ( T )(2ei + ηi ). In a dis- 6
7    counted finite-state game, Theorem 3 gives (2δi + ϵi )/(1 − qi ). These are player- 7
8    specific bounds; a sum of critic losses is not a substitute. They remain valid without       8

9    uniqueness or convergence of the simultaneous policy-update dynamics, provided               9

10   the reported profile satisfies the stated errors and best-response comparison hy- 10
11   potheses.                                                                                    11

12     The static Cournot diagnostic makes the distinction visible. With payoffs qi (1 −          12

13   qi − q j ), symmetric Nash output is 1/3 per firm. Joint-profit maximization gives           13

14   a symmetric allocation 1/4. At the latter profile, a firm optimally deviates to 3/8,         14

15   earning a gain of 1/64. A joint objective can therefore be perfectly optimized and           15

16   still fail a unilateral equilibrium test. This arithmetic is included as a mandatory         16

17   regression check.                                                                            17

18                                                                                                18

19                                                                                                19
                                34.2. Symmetry and market scaling
20                                                                                                20

21     Identical initial weights do not prove equilibrium uniqueness or permutation               21

22   equivariance. An equivariant actor can be specified using own capital and a                  22

23   permutation-invariant aggregation of rival states. Even then, one must check unilat- 23
24   eral deviations rather than infer equilibrium from architectural symmetry. Multiple          24

25   starting profiles and unsuccessful best-response searches belong in a numerical              25

26   report.                                                                                      26

27     Under fixed market size, positive constant capital per firm cannot be called               27

28   the competitive limit while total output tends to infinity and the truncated price           28

29   tends to zero. Maintaining that capital entails investment and adjustment costs. 29
30   Under M N = N, positive capital per firm may instead be compatible with a growing            30

31   market, but it describes a different sequence of economies. The original unsupported         31

32   capital table remains in the archive. Section 34.3 replaces its evidentiary role with a      32
                                       NBO SUPPLEMENT                                      97

1    fully specified finite dynamic game and independent best-response calculations,            1

2    without asserting convergence of unrestricted neural game training.                        2

3                                                                                               3

4                                                                                               4
               34.3. Neural dynamic capital policies and full unilateral deviations
5                                                                                               5

6      For two firms, we retain the capital game with A = 1, P0 = 2, δ = .1, σK = .15,          6

7    ρ = .04, T = 3, investment in [0, .8], and capital in [.05, 1.5]2 . Terminal or joint- 7
8    domain exit pays .5Ki . Baseline adjustment coefficients are b1 = b2 = 1; the earlier      8

9    asymmetric experiment has b2 = 1.2. The principal neural grid has 17 × 17 states,          9

10   30 time steps, and 11 actions per player. Positive cubature and interpolation define       10

11   the finite game. Market normalizations M2 = 1 and M2 = 2 are distinct economies,           11

12   not evidence for an uncomputed large-population limit.                                     12

13     At each time and state, the R7 calculation trains separate critics and actors. It        13

14   constructs both full 11 × 11 continuation-payoff matrices. The training labels are         14

15   a pure one-step equilibrium when one exists and a minimum-maximum-defect                   15

16   pair otherwise. The actor’s own objective is differentiated with the rival action and      16

17   continuation critic fixed. Thus this study actually trains neural strategic policies,      17

18   but it also pays for an exhaustive joint-action training oracle. Raw policies, three- 18
19   by-three shortlist policies, and any corrected policies are distinct stored arrays.        19

20     After freezing the entire profile, an independent backward optimization permits          20

21   every own action at every state and time against the fixed rival policy. Its value         21

22   minus the profile payoff is the unilateral exploitability map. This is not a one- 22
23   period test with the approximate continuation held fixed. The raw and final profiles       23

24   meet the .1 absolute-profit target in every principal R7 case; the full-oracle guard       24

25   is unused in those cases. Five state-time nodes across the six neural runs lack            25

26   a pure one-step equilibrium for the fitted continuation matrices. Their locations          26

27   and defects are reported in the supplement. A minimum-defect selection at those            27

28   nodes is not called an exact equilibrium. Nevertheless, the complete dynamic               28

29   best-response bound remains valid for the resulting profile: its proof does not            29

30   assume that every fitted one-step game has a pure solution. Moreover, for fixed            30

31   Markov rivals, randomized or history-dependent own deviations cannot improve               31

32   on the finite Markov best-response value, because each conditional action mixture          32

16     R8 adds actor-free neural continuation fitting and classical backward selection for                   16

17   both market normalizations. The neural comparator allocates 480 critic Adam up- 17
18   dates per time level rather than R7’s 320 critic plus 160 actor updates, with the same                  18

19   L-BFGS cap. Counts and timings reveal the difference between nominal update                             19

20   matching and matched wall time. Both methods receive their own independent                              20

21   full dynamic best responses. The earlier six classical game cases, asymmetric costs,                    21

22   alternative tie rules, boundary frequencies, coarse counterexample, and finer-grid                      22

23   results remain in the supplement and raw record. The finite-game result is a nu- 23
24   merically evaluated approximate equilibrium with a specified profit-unit bound. It                      24

25   is not inferred from stationarity of a summed actor loss or the static 1/64 diagnos- 25
26   tic. State interpolation, continuous investment, diffusion timing, and continuously                     26

27   monitored exits remain separate terms in a continuous-game error account.                               27

1    laboratory uses a Lyapunov solve for this critic and exact actor improvement. It is            1

2    an exactly representable NBO subfamily and a matrix-identity test, not evidence                2

3    that generic stochastic neural optimization has the same convergence rate.                     3

4      Across dimensions 2, 5, 10, 20, 50 and three stabilizing actor initializations, the          4

5    block iteration takes six steps. The maximum error in P, in operator norm, is below            5

6    10−13 relative to an independent algebraic Riccati solver. The policy-evaluation               6

7    coefficient residual is below 10−12 . On the unit Euclidean ball, the actor gap is             7

8    bounded by ∥K − P∥22 , and the residual bound follows from the operator norm of                8

21     Both networks have two hidden tanh layers of width thirty-two. The critic en- 21
22   forces the terminal payoff exactly. The main design uses three seeds at d = 2, 5, 10, 20,                      22

23   600 Adam updates, and batches of 128 independently sampled state–time points. 23
24   Exact automatic differentiation supplies the trace. The held-out set contains 512                              24

25   fixed independent points in [0, 1] × [−.5, .5]d . A common diagnostic target is resid-                         25

26   ual root mean square at most .025 and maximum queried action gap at most .01. 26
27   The recorded first crossing is not necessarily sustained; final failures are also re- 27
28   ported. The experiment measures real neural approximation and optimization costs                               28

29   rather than exact quadratic identities. It also shows why a diagnostic target is not                           29

30   a welfare certificate. Simulated policy paths can leave the fitted state box; Euler                            30

31   policy-payoff estimates from the initial state need not agree with a critic that has a                         31

32   small sampled residual inside that box. A follow-up expands the fitting and verifi- 32
     102

1    cation box to [−1.5, .5]d and uses 1,000 updates at dimensions ten and twenty. Both      1

2    designs, paired policy-payoff samples, and forty/eighty-step simulation outputs          2

3    are retained. Sampling standard errors do not remove time-discretization bias or         3

4    certify an optimum. Process high-water memory is labeled as such, not interpreted        4

5    as an isolated per-network allocation. No high-dimensional uniform residual bound        5

                   35.2. Method-paired payoff evidence and arithmetic replay
9                                                                                             9

10     The R7 follow-up evaluates the coupled-capital policies at dimensions ten and          10

11   twenty, for all three fixed seeds, both fitting domains, and 40, 80, and 160 Euler       11

12   steps. The same 512 Brownian paths are used by NBO and the direct compara- 12
13   tor within every cell. For path j, define D j = JjNBO − Jjdirect . We report D and the   13
                                                   √
14   pointwise Student interval D ± t511,.975 s D / 512. Pairing removes the irrelevant       14

15   component of common path variation from the comparison. The complete set of 36           15

16   comparisons is reproduced from the pinned raw arrays in the supplement. The old          16

17   twenty-dimensional fitting box is exited by essentially all recorded paths. Wider- 17
18   domain fitting eliminates observed exits in the recorded audits and changes both         18

19   payoff agreement and method differences. Zero observed exits in 512 paths is not a       19

20   zero exit-probability theorem. Likewise, these intervals quantify pointwise Monte        20

21   Carlo uncertainty under a specified Euler scheme. They are neither simultaneous          21

22   confidence statements across all 36 comparisons nor bounds on time bias, optimal         22

23   value, or the global differential residual. The experiment identifies a consequential    23

24   coverage failure and its measured correction without redefining the unbounded            24

25   economic state space as a stopped box.                                                   25

26     The one-state consumption certificate has a different evidentiary status. R7 inde- 26
27   pendently replays its previously generated complete leaf cover with mpmath.iv at         27

28   40 decimal digits, without importing the original interval arithmetic implementa- 28
29   tion. For the stored seed-11 networks, the NBO policy-loss bound is about .04961         29

30   and the direct-HJB bound about .04967. Both meet the stated threshold. This is an        30

31   independent interval-arithmetic replay on the previously generated complete cover, not   31

32   independent network training, independent cover generation, or a formal machine          32

                            35.3. Exact and stochastic trace computation
24                                                                                                 24

25     Let Σ include any covariance factor and set B = Σ⊤ D2 vΣ. For independent probes            25

26   with identity covariance,                                                                     26

27                                                                                                 27
                                                         1 K
28                                 q = tr( B),       qb = ∑ z⊤   Bz .                       (27)   28
                                                         K k =1 k k
29                                                                                                 29

30   Gaussian probes have variance 2∥ B∥2F /K. If r = d − q/2, then                                30

31                                                                                                 31

32                                  E(d − qb/2)2 = r2 + 41 Var(qb).                         (28)   32
     104

1    The extra term depends on the critic. Unbiased trace estimation therefore does not      1

2    give an unbiased squared-residual objective.                                            2

3      Two conditionally independent probe banks repair this particular bias:                3

4                                                                                            4

5                                                                                            5
                                  (1)       (2)
6                     L = (d − qb /2)(d − qb /2),
                      b                                 E[ b               2
                                                           L | t, x, ξ ] = r .        (29)   6

7                                                                                            7

8                                                                                            8

9    With dominated differentiation, its gradient is also unbiased. The sample product       9

10   need not be nonnegative and can have substantial variance, so this is an objective      10

11   identity rather than a guarantee of stable optimization. Exact low-dimensional          11

12   derivatives remain the independent check. If the actor or deterministic term depends    12

13   on the same probes, conditional independence must be re-established; the simple         13

14   identity cannot be reused automatically.                                                14

15     The relevant computational cost depends on the network evaluation and differ- 15
16   entiation cost, Brownian rank, covariance factorization, sample count, optimization     16

17   iterations, and requested accuracy. Forming a dense Hessian involves quadratic          17

18   storage; Hessian-vector products can avoid it. Neither operation count by itself is a   18

19   total-complexity theorem. Active control bounds do not universally create gradient      19

20   discontinuities, and a noisy trace cannot make a correctly projected box action leave   20

21   its box. Feasibility and value-derivative accuracy are separate diagnostics.            21

22     The independent-bank estimator is also exercised during actual training in the        22

23   coupled ten-state economy. One implementation uses two independent banks of             23

24   two Gaussian Hessian-vector probes; the matched single-bank ablation squares a          24

25   four-probe estimate. Exact traces are used only for the common held-out diagnostics. 25
26   All three seeds, objective signs, optimization histories, and policy-payoff samples     26

27   are saved. The two training procedures perform similarly at the retained covariance     27

28   scale; unbiasedness alone does not establish a finite-budget advantage. A separate      28

29   Monte Carlo test checks both the product objective and its parameter gradient           29

30   against exact trace expressions with declared tolerances. These calculations validate   30

31   the estimator’s execution without identifying a random training objective with a        31

32   deterministic certificate.                                                              32

1                   36. VALIDATED EVALUATION AND ACTION IMPROVEMENT                                 1

2                                                                                                   2
       The neural Bellman map must account for two distinct approximation decisions:
3
     which continuation values are passed to the next stage, and how tightly the admis- 3
4                                                                                                   4
     sible action supremum is bounded. This section supplies an explicit output rule
5                                                                                                   5
     for the first decision and a signed enclosure for the second. Neither construction
6                                                                                                   6
     presumes that a stationary point of the training loss is economically optimal. Both
7                                                                                                   7
     apply to the original preference economy and leave its controls, state law, and
8                                                                                                   8
     stopping convention unchanged.
9                                                                                                   9

4                                                                                                                4

5         The guard gives an implementable meaning to the evaluation component of a                              5

6    vanishing-perturbation condition. It does not supply the action-improvement error,                          6

7    the consistency error of a stopped diffusion approximation, or convergence of an un- 7
8    corrected network away from the nodes. Its cost can be substantial: in the numerical                        8

9    economy it corrects a large fraction of fitted nodal values. We consequently count                          9

10   the correction memory and compare against an actor-free method with the identical                           10

11   guard. The construction is not evidence of neural compression when almost every                             11

12   node is retained.                                                                                           12

14                           36.2. Signed enclosures for continuous actions                                      14

15                                                                                                               15
          Fix a state-time node and a continuation vector w. Write Qw ( a) for its one-step
16                                                                                                               16
     payoff. The action set is the full compact box of the preference economy. Let B = [ℓ, r ]
17                                                                                                               17
     be an action rectangle with centre c. A natural interval calculation supplies an upper
18                                                                                                               18
     bound U nat ( B), while independent feasible evaluations supply a lower incumbent
19                                                                                                               19
     L.
20                                                                                                               20
          The continuation is bilinear within state cells and continuous across their internal
21                                                                                                               21
     faces. Reflection changes its one-sided slopes. A stopped transition is different:
22                                                                                                               22
     the implementation switches between interpolated continuation and the specified
23                                                                                                               23
     stopping payoff, which need not agree away from boundary grid points. We call B
24                                                                                                               24
     regular when every cubature transition stays entirely inside, or entirely outside, each
25                                                                                                               25
     wealth stopping face. Only on such a box do we use derivative information. All
26                                                                                                               26
     intersected interpolation cells and both sides of reflection folds enter the derivative
27                                                                                                               27
     hull.
28                                                                                                               28
          Suppose Gj ( B) = [ g−     +
                               j , g j ] encloses every one-sided partial derivative with respect
29                                                                                                               29
     to action component j on a regular box. The mean-value upper enclosure is

       T HEOREM 3—Signed action cover: Assume that the natural enclosures contain their                7

8
     respective payoffs, that the derivative hulls contain all one-sided derivatives on each regular   8

9
     box, and that feasible point values and arithmetic bounds are outward. Start from a cover of      9

10
     the action box and apply subdivision, reduction to a verified maximizing face, and removal        10

11
     of a box only when its upper bound is at most L + ε. At any interruption, retain the upper        11

12
     bound of every unresolved box. Then                                                               12

13                                                                                                     13
                           sup Qw ( a) ≤ max{ L + ε,        max        U ( B)}.                (32)
14                         a∈A                          B unresolved                                   14

15
     The bound remains valid when a depth, work, or time budget is exhausted. If r action coordi- 15
16                                                                                                     16
     nates have verified strict derivative signs throughout a regular parent box, maximization
17                                                                                                     17
     over that box reduces to a face of dimension at most d a − r.
18                                                                                                     18

19     The last statement describes a source of computational improvement, not a                       19

20   dimension-free worst-case bound. Under a K-Lipschitz gradient, a regular stationary               20

21   box of diameter s has mean-value excess of order Ks2 when its point evaluation                    21

22   error is negligible. Boxes that cross stopping switches, have ambiguous derivative                22

23   signs, or contain many interpolation cells can remain expensive. Our experiment                   23

24   measures their actual cost and keeps unresolved bounds; it does not assume that                   24

25   every box has favorable structure.                                                                25

26     The operational NBO output is now explicit: the frozen policy representation,                   26

27   its guarded continuation vector when used, an independently propagated policy                     27

28   interval, and a complete-action optimal upper envelope. A nodal lookup policy is                  28

29   the object certified in the preference experiment. An off-grid interpolation of that              29

30   policy is a different deployment rule and is evaluated separately. No online search               30

31   is charged to a stored lookup table; constructing that table includes every actor,                31

32   critic, and local-search query.                                                                   32
