1                      37. COST, REFINEMENT, AND ECONOMIC ACCURACY                               1

2                                                                                                2
       The preceding applications define the original economic scope of NBO. We now
3                                                                                                3
     evaluate the guarded map and signed cover on the same preference economy, and
4                                                                                                4
     connect the high-dimensional experiment to an absolute economic benchmark. The
5                                                                                                5
     comparison retains every earlier failure. In particular, a new upper envelope does
6                                                                                                6
     not turn an actor-plus-search policy into an actor-only policy, and retraining on a
7                                                                                                7
     finer grid does not repair the off-grid performance of a previously frozen policy.
8                                                                                                8

9                                                                                                9
                       37.1. A tighter certificate for identical frozen policies
10                                                                                               10

11     The reference specification has 17 × 25 state nodes, twenty dates, and the continu- 11
12   ous action box [0.02, 0.30] × [0, 1.5] × [−0.45, 0.45]. The ratios constrain consumption,   12

13   leverage, and preference adjustment in the declared economy. They are economic              13

14   primitives, not bounds on an unconstrained optimum. Active consumption or port- 14
15   folio limits remain meaningful features of this constrained specification; changing         15

16   them changes the economy.                                                                   16

17     We freeze the nine previously computed actor-plus-search, direct-plus-search,             17

18   and search-only policies, including their original seeds. The complete-action upper         18

19   envelope is recomputed independently of these policies’ lower payoff envelopes. 19
20   The new cover uses a one-step tolerance of 0.0002 and retains every unresolved              20

21   upper bound. Its output can be shared across policies only because their action set,        21

22   transition rule, domain, and payoff normalization coincide. Table XLI reports the           22

23   resulting all-date, all-node loss bounds. The improvement relative to the historical        23

24   table concerns the tightness of the verification calculation, not additional training or    24

25   a change in preferences. The raw actors remain separately reported in the machine- 25
26   readable common accounts. Search remains consequential, and these results do not            26

27   establish an actor advantage over the actor-free comparator. They do show that the          27

28   earlier failure of a 0.1 upper-bound target need not imply that the frozen hybrid           28

29   policies themselves are more than 0.1 from the nodal optimum.                               29

30     For a controlled cost comparison, Table XLII gives one-step corner-only and               30

31   signed covers at three dates, with identical continuation arrays, initial feasible          31

32   actions, tolerance, depth limit, and work budget. Wall time includes derivative hull        32

                       37.2. The whole-domain refinement failure and its repair
24                                                                                                              24

25     A frozen policy has to be distinguished from a policy refitted on a new grid. The                        25

26   complete historical refinement arrays reveal large losses away from the centre. Their                      26

27   maximum positive reference advantages, locations, quantiles, boundary-region                               27

28   summaries, and action saturation rates are now included in the supplement. The                             28

29   largest historical discrepancy is approximately 2.1234 at high risk aversion and                           29

30   wealth close to the lower stopping boundary. Centre values alone do not summarize                          30

31   that experiment. These discrepancies are feasible-reference lower bounds on policy                         31

32   regret, not upper bounds for the continuous economy.                                                       32

1    interpolated from the coarse certificate. It is applied to all twelve fine-grid policies,   1

2    including the six unguarded runs. This separates three facts: the failure of a frozen       2

3    coarse-grid policy; the accuracy achieved by retraining within a declared nodal             3

4    economy; and the additional error required to transfer a result to a continuously           4

5    monitored diffusion. The first remains in the record, the second is measured, and the       5

6    third is not assigned a zero value. Two levels alone do not establish an asymptotic         6

7    convergence rate.                                                                           7

8      The deployment specification is a stored state-time action table with its saved           8

9    neural proposal and continuation correction. Evaluating this table at its nodes             9

10   requires no online maximizing search. Interpolating the table at off-grid states            10

11   invokes a different policy rule; the historical experiment shows why this distinction       11

12   matters. A raw actor without a table or local improvement has a different memory            12

13   requirement and is not represented by the hybrid loss bounds.                               13

14                                                                                               14

15                                                                                               15

16                                                                                               16
                              37.3. A model-specific welfare account
17                                                                                               17

18     An absolute utility gap is not a consumption-equivalent percentage when the               18

19   utility exponent is itself a controlled state. We therefore compute a defined com- 19
20   pensating transfer. Hold the saved policy and its state law fixed, leave preference- 20
21   adjustment costs and the stopping bequest unchanged, and add externally financed            21

22   consumption so that flow consumption becomes (1 + e)ct Xt . This intervention               22

23   changes felicity but does not charge its financing to wealth. It is a compensation          23

24   experiment, not another admissible self-financed policy in the original box.                24

25     Let Jeπ be the compensated payoff and U the common optimal upper envelope. 25
26   A verified lower envelope J π
                                 e
                                   ≥ U at every active starting node and date implies 26
27   that this transfer compensates the policy’s loss everywhere in the declared nodal           27

28   economy. We search for a common e using monotonicity of CRRA felicity in con- 28
29   sumption and verify the final inequality by outward propagation. Table XLIV gives           29

30   the resulting upper percentages. These conservative transfers can be economically           30

31   substantial even when the absolute utility bound is below 0.1; the threshold is not         31

32   described as a universally small welfare loss.                                              32

                      37.4. A global benchmark for the coupled capital economy
19                                                                                                             19

20     A comparison of two frozen policies does not indicate how far either is from                            20

21   the economic optimum. We supplement the paired simulations by a bound on the                              21

22   original, continuous-time, dense nonlinear capital economy. It does not rely on                           22

23   a Riccati equation, separability of the optimal policy, a state mesh, or a sampled                        23

24   residual.                                                                                                 24

28   σC2 )/2. Common noise affects the mean but cancels from cross-sectional dispersion. 28

19     The bound exploits bounded nonlinear drift, concavity of consumption utility,                19

20   and an explicit feasible policy. Its numerical integration uses outward rectangle              20

21   sums, interval exponential and logarithm evaluations, and square roots checked                 21

22   by interval squaring. Table XLV reports the computed brackets. Their width is                  22

23   economically material and is not presented as a small regret bound for a learned               23

24   policy. They nevertheless provide an absolute benchmark absent from a paired- 24
25   policy comparison. Simulations of m0 use the same Brownian paths as the saved                  25

26   neural policies. The resulting differences and confidence intervals concern their              26

27   common Euler discretization; an unknown simulation bias is not subtracted from                 27

28   the analytical bound to create a neural-policy certificate.                                    28

                             38.1. A fixed protocol with strong comparators
18                                                                                                              18

19     Each fit uses 600 critic updates, batch size 128, and the same state draws and                           19

20   initialization seeds across methods. The critic learning rate is .0015; the actor learn- 20
21   ing rate is .002. Two independent Gaussian trace banks, with two probes each,                              21

22   are actually used in the product residual objective. Negative sample losses are re- 22
23   tained. Training states have t ∈ [0, 1] and each log capital in [−1.5, .5]. Exact-Hessian                  23

24   diagnostics at 128 held-out points do not enter the continuous certificate.                                24

25     The direct-tube comparator removes the actor and globally maximizes the same                             25

26   critic Hamiltonian on the identical time-dependent tube. The direct-full comparator                        26

27   instead maximizes on the full primitive box [.02, 2]d . Both exploit the scalar mono- 27
28   tone mean-consumption equation and use 44 bisection steps. They receive the same                           28

29   critic architecture, sample stream, trace banks, and update count. Their additional                        29

30   maximization work is counted. This is a matched-update design, not a claim that                            30

31   unequal wall times are equal compute budgets. The untrained feasible schedule m0                           31

32   is a fourth comparator.                                                                                    32
                                            NBO SUPPLEMENT                                          115

1      There are 27 fitted policies, including all seeds and both direct alternatives. Every               1

2    saved actor is deployed without local action search. The full deployment cost                         2

3    includes the inward scalar schedule guard; direct deployment also includes critic                     3

4    differentiation and bisection. Separate process runs record peak resident memory,                     4

5    training time, query counts, and optimizer histories. The raw proposals, guarded                      5

6    deployment routines, and the scalar benchmark are not treated as interchangeable                      6

7    policies.                                                                                             7

                          38.3. What is learned, and what is guaranteed
21                                                                                                21

22     Table XLVII reports the actual cost of training and deploying the policies. The            22

23   supplement reports every seed, rather than only an average of successful cases. The          23

24   structural certificate already holds for the initial zero-output actor; therefore the        24

25   time to its first certificate is not its training time. The anchor has a tighter guarantee   25

26   and no neural training cost. These facts are central to the interpretation, not omitted      26

27   ablations.                                                                                   27

25     This experiment supplies a trained multilayer NBO policy, a strong direct com- 25
26   parator, a deployed-policy specification, and an independent continuous-economy                                 26

27   guarantee in one coupled nonlinear model. It does not establish that the actor is uni- 27
28   versally preferable to a structured feasible schedule, that constant-step optimization                          28

29   converges, or that the same certificate applies to endogenous preference formation. 29

                               39. PRIOR CONCLUDING DISCUSSION (R10)
19                                                                                                             19

20     Neural Bellman Operators organize computation around policy evaluation, feasi- 20
21   ble improvement, and independent economic verification. Recursive utility, endoge- 21
22   nous preferences, sophisticated temporal selves, and dynamic strategic interaction                        22

23   remain part of that economic formulation. The representation is neural, but an                            23

24   economic claim concerns the policy actually deployed and the error that has been                          24

25   bounded.                                                                                                  25

26     The guarded map specifies how a nodal evaluation tolerance is enforced and                              26

27   records the indexed corrections it requires. Signed derivative enclosures reduce                          27

28   complete-action verification work without dropping unresolved boxes or differen- 28
29   tiating across a stopping discontinuity. Their numerical contribution is identified                       29

30   using identical frozen policies and matched one-step comparisons. Fresh training                          30

31   and recertification on genuinely nested grids are kept distinct from the full-domain                      31

32   failure of interpolated coarse policies. The compensation account translates util- 32
                                          NBO SUPPLEMENT                                     119

1    ity accuracy into a stated financed-transfer experiment rather than a misleading               1

2    common percentage.                                                                             2

3      The dense capital experiment now has a feasible-policy lower benchmark and                   3

4    a continuous-time optimal upper benchmark on the original state space. Those                   4

5    historical absolute brackets are distinct from the new continuous-time neural-policy           5

6    bound in Theorem 7. The latter uses the original capital economy and full adapted              6

7    comparison class, with a schedule-centred actor architecture. Paired simulations               7

8    remain discretized policy comparisons. Likewise, the complete-action preference                8

9    certificates do not yet bound all continuous-state, continuous-time, and stopping- 9
10   monitoring defects. The evidence does not establish that actors outperform the                 10

11   strongest low-dimensional search and projection alternatives; those comparisons                11

12   remain in the record. The contribution is a specified neural Bellman computation               12

13   with verified evaluation and improvement outputs, measured costs, and separate                 13

14   economic domains for each conclusion.                                                          14

15                                                                                                  15

16                                   APPENDIX A: P ROOFS                                            16

17                                                                                                  17
                                      A.1. Proof of Theorem 2
18                                                                                                  18
       Extend the aggregator Lipschitz-continuously outside its invariant interval for
19                                                                                                  19
     this comparison argument. The extension leaves all policy utilities and the candi-
20                                                                                                  20
     date residual unchanged. For a nonnegative number c, define dc (t) = Be L(T −t) +
21                                                                                                  21
     cA L ( T − t). Then dc ( T ) = B, dc (t) ≥ B, and −d′c = Ldc + c. Since a spatially constant
22                                                                                                  22
     shift leaves the generator unchanged and f is L-Lipschitz in utility,
23                                                                                                  23

24
     F [v + dc ] ≥ F [v] − d′c − Ldc = F [v] + c,    F [v − dc ] ≤ F [v] + d′c + Ldc = F [v] − c.   24

25                                                                                                  25

26   Because F [v] = r a [v] − g a [v] ∈ [−e − η, e], v + de+η is a supersolution and v − de+η      26

27   a subsolution. They dominate the required stopping data on every boundary face. 27
28   A spatially constant shift also preserves an exactly satisfied homogeneous reflected           28

29   boundary condition. Comparison proves (15).                                                    29

30     Apply the same argument to the fixed-policy operator F a [v] = −vt − H a [v], with           30

31   c = e, to obtain (14). The triangle inequality gives (16), and policy admissibility            31

32   gives its nonnegative lower bound. The construction uses L ≥ 0 and dc (t) ≥ B                  32
     120

1    specifically to cover lateral stopping boundaries; it does not incorrectly discount         1

2    their errors as if every path stopped only at T. For nonsmooth candidates the same          2

3    argument applies when their residual inequalities are verified in the viscosity sense. 3
4    The theorem’s differential implementation, however, requires the stated smooth              4

5    candidate.                                                                       Q.E.D.     5

6                                                                                                6

7                                   A.2. Exact policy improvement                                7

8
       Suppose v = V a solves the fixed-policy problem and a feasible ã satisfies               8

9
     H ã [v] ≥ H a [v] pointwise. Then F ã [v] ≤ 0 with the same boundary payoff. Policy       9

10
     comparison gives V ã ≥ V a . This is a policy-space statement. A single gradient step      10

11
     on a domain-averaged actor loss does not automatically meet its pointwise premise. 11
12                                                                                               12
     The measured action gap and policy-evaluation errors are retained precisely because
13                                                                                               13
     approximate neural improvement can violate that premise.
14                                                                                               14

     The same monotonicity implies order preservation. Taking the maximum over
21                                                                                               21

22                                                                                               22
     ment supplies the two fixed points on the invariant complete value domain.
23                                                                                               23
       Now ∥v − vha ∥ ≤ ∥v − Tha v∥ + qh ∥v − vha ∥, which gives the first bound. Also ∥v −

                                       A.4. Proof of Theorem 4
29                                                                                               29

30     First, (18) gives                                                                         30

31                                           δh + ϵh   1 + λh δh + ϵh                            31
                            ∥vh − v∗h ∥∞ ≤           =                −→ 0.
32                                           1 − qh       λ      h                               32
                                       NBO SUPPLEMENT                                      121

1    It remains to identify the limit of the exact monotone scheme. Stability makes the up- 1
2    per and lower half-relaxed limits finite. At a strict local maximum of the upper limit      2

3    minus a smooth test function, select nearby maximizing grid points. Monotonicity            3

4    compares the neighboring scheme values to that test function plus the vanishing             4

5    contact offset. Local consistency then gives the viscosity subsolution inequality. 5
6    The corresponding minima give the supersolution inequality for the lower limit. 6
7    Consistent boundary data and the assumed barriers give the required boundary                7

8    inequalities. Comparison orders the upper limit below the lower limit. The reverse          8

9    order follows by definition, so the two limits coincide with the unique continuous          9

10   viscosity solution. The vanishing distance between vh and v∗h transfers that conver- 10
11   gence to the neural arrays. The discrete policy-value conclusion follows from the           11

12   third bound. Interpreting a discrete policy as a continuous-time implementation             12

13   additionally requires convergence of its controlled interpolation and payoff; the           13

14   theorem does not infer this merely from convergence of its state arrays. This is the        14

15   usual monotone-scheme selection argument, with the neural approximation error               15

16   made explicit; see Barles and Souganidis (1991), Crandall et al. (1992).        Q.E.D.      16

       Let Tn and Tna be the time-n Bellman and fixed-policy maps, with Lipschitz
21                                                                                               21
     constants qn (which may exceed one). For arrays vn , define δn = ∥vn − Tna vn+1 ∥ and
22                                                                                               22
     ϵn = ∥ Tn vn+1 − Tna vn+1 ∥. If the terminal error is BN , backward induction gives
23                                                                                               23

24                                          !                    !                               24
                                     N −1          N −1   k −1
25                   ∥vn − v∗n ∥ ≤   ∏ qj       BN + ∑    ∏ qj       (δk + ϵk ).                 25

26                                   j=n            k=n   j=n                                    26

27                                                                                               27

28   The policy-evaluation bound replaces δk + ϵk by δk . Their sum bounds regret. Empty         28

29   products equal one. For the implicit Lipschitz recursive step, qn ≤ (1 − hL)−1 ; for        29

30   additive exponential discount, qn = e−ρh . Thus a per-step error o (h) and consis- 30
31   tent terminal data suffice on a fixed finite horizon, without asserting a stationary        31

32   contraction in an inapplicable utility regime.                                              32

19            APPENDIX B: P ROOFS FOR THE G UARDED AND S IGNED M AP                                                19

20                                                                                                                 20
                                        B.1. Proof of Proposition 2
21                                                                                                                 21

22     At a replaced node the output equals the computed target. At any other node its                             22

23   distance from that target is at most τh . The triangle inequality gives the one-step                          23

24   conclusion. For a frozen policy sequence, let en be the sup-norm difference between                           24

25   the guarded and exact policy values at date n. Positivity of the transition weights                           25

26   and their mass bound imply en ≤ τh + ηh + qh en+1 , with e N = 0 for exact terminal                           26

27   data. Backward induction gives the geometric sum. As N = T/h and qh ≤ 1, the                                  27

28   bound is at most T (0.1h + ηh /h) under the stated schedule. No property of the                               28

29   optimizer was used. The assertion concerns the guarded nodal values. An off-node                              29

30   neural interpolant, an improved policy, and a diffusion approximation each require                            30

31   their separate error account. In fixed-precision arithmetic, ηh = o (h) is a maintained                       31

32   refinement condition, not a fact that can be inferred as h tends to zero indefinitely.□                       32
                                       NBO SUPPLEMENT                                   123

1                                   B.2. Proof of Theorem 3                                    1

2                                                                                              2

3      On a regular box, the payoff restricted to a line segment is continuous and piece- 3
4    wise continuously differentiable, with finitely many interpolation and reflection         4

5    knots, or is the limit of such segments. Integration of its almost-everywhere deriva- 5
6    tive yields                                                                               6

7                                                                                              7

8                                                                                              8

9                                         Z 1                                                  9
                     Qw ( a) − Qw (c) =         ∇ Qw (c + s( a − c)) · ( a − c) ds.
10                                         0                                                   10

11                                                                                             11

12                                                                                             12

13   Every partial derivative is included in its interval hull. Maximizing the resulting       13

14   interval products proves (31). Taking the minimum with another valid upper en- 14
15   closure preserves validity. If a partial derivative is strictly positive throughout the   15

16   box, integration along that coordinate shows that replacing it by its upper endpoint      16

17   cannot lower the payoff; the negative case is analogous. Repeating the argument           17

18   proves face domination and the dimension statement.                                       18

19     A subdivision replaces a parent by children whose union contains it. A face             19

20   reduction replaces a set by a subset with the same supremum. Removal occurs               20

21   only after that supremum has a bound no larger than the incumbent plus tolerance. 21
22   Feasible point evaluations can only increase the incumbent, so an earlier removed         22

23   box stays bounded by the final incumbent plus tolerance. The remaining boxes              23

24   keep their upper bounds without any success inference from an interrupted search. 24
25   Induction over all operations proves (32). At a stopping switch the line-segment          25

26   continuity premise can fail; the implementation therefore uses the natural union          26

27   enclosure and does not invoke the derivative argument there.                              27

28     For the local quadratic observation, a K-Lipschitz gradient with a zero-gradient        28

29   point in the box has norm at most Ks throughout a box of diameter s. Integration          29

30   over a segment of length at most s bounds the variation by Ks2 . This conditional         30

31   observation does not extend to an arbitrary switching box, nor does it assert that all    31

32   nodes possess such a box.                                                            □    32

                                       B.4. Proof of Proposition 4
23                                                                                           23

24
       Let m = d−1   ∑i mi . Jensen’s inequality gives d−1 ∑i log mi ≤ log m. Moreover, 24
25
     d−1 ∑i tanh(( BY )i ) ≤ 1. The mean-state drift is consequently no larger than b+ − m. 25
26
     The dispersion penalty at the terminal date is nonnegative and can be dropped           26

27
     for an upper bound. Fubini’s theorem applied to the integrated expected mean            27

28
     state and terminal mean state yields the kernel w(t). The Brownian terms have           28

29
     zero mean; bounded controls and bounded nonlinear drift ensure the required             29

30
     integrability. Thus every admissible policy has expected payoff at most                 30

31
                     Z T                                                                     31
                           e−ρt max {log m − ζm2 /2 + w(t)(b+ − m)} dt.
32                     0        m∈[m,m]                                                      32

1    The integrand is strictly concave in m. Its derivative is 1/m − ζm − w(t), whose            1

2    positive root gives m0 (t) before clipping. This proves the upper bound.                    2

3      For the feasible common schedule, the expected mean-state drift is at least               3

4    b− − m0 (t). Define the normalized dispersion norm ∥ Z ∥22,d = E[d−1 ∑i Zi2 ]. Cen- 4
5    tred common controls and common noise vanish from the dispersion equation. For              5

6    numbers in [−1, 1], their cross-sectional variance is at most one. Hence Minkowski’s        6

7    inequality bounds the norm of the integrated centred nonlinear drift by κT. The 7
                                                    p
8    centred idiosyncratic Brownian part has norm σI (1 − 1/d) T. A second applica- 8
9    tion of Minkowski and squaring give the upper bound Dd on expected terminal                 9

10   dispersion. Subtracting χe−ρT D      d from the lower mean-payoff calculation proves        10

11   J (m0 ) ≥ Ld . Feasibility gives J (m0 ) ≤ V ∗ . The derivation never restricts the dense   11

12   matrix B beyond the stated bounded-tanh form.                                         □     12

15                                                                                               15
                            B.5. Compensation and arithmetic scope
16                                                                                               16

17     For each realized state and positive consumption, CRRA felicity is increasing             17

18   in consumption, including when u > 1 and its level is negative. The financed sup- 18
19   plement leaves the state law and every other payoff term unchanged. Its expected            19

20   payoff is therefore nondecreasing in e. Positivity of the nodal transition operator         20

21   propagates a lower interval payoff at each date. If its lower endpoint exceeds the          21

22   optimal upper envelope at every active starting node and date, the corresponding            22

23   financed supplement compensates the loss throughout that nodal economy. Bisec- 23
24   tion searches for a sufficient supplement; only the final verified inequality provides      24

25   the stated upper bound.                                                                     25

26     All implemented interval conclusions use the inherited binary64 execution con- 26
27   tract, with outward elementary operations and separately bounded transcendental             27

28   evaluations. They concern exact real interpretations of stored primitive coefficients       28

29   and grid nodes. They are not a formal verification of the interpreter, hardware,            29

30   or all library internals. In the capital bound, square-root seeds are accepted only         30

31   after interval squaring verifies both endpoint inequalities, and the time integral is       31

32   bounded by interval rectangles rather than by an adaptive quadrature tolerance. 32
     126

1    Random stress tests are regression evidence, not substitutes for these inclusion              1

2    arguments.                                                                                    2

15                                                                                                                          15

16                         C.4. Interval spectral and deployment checks                                                     16

17     For the stored exact-real matrix B, outward products and sums enclose each entry                                     17

18   of B⊤ B. The Schur complement recurrence for an LDL⊤ factorization is enclosed                                         18

19   entry by entry. If every pivot interval has strictly positive lower endpoint, every                                    19
                                                                               √
20   exact pivot is positive, so λI − B⊤ B is positive definite. Hence ∥ B∥2 < λ. The                                       20

21   proposed numerical singular value is not part of this proof; an unsuccessful interval                                  21

22   factorization supplies no spectral bound.                                                                              22

23     If ℓ ≤ m0 (t) ≤ u, then up(u − ϵ) ≥ m0 (t) − ϵ and down(ℓ + ϵ) ≤ m0 (t) + ϵ. Every                                   23

24   finite output after projection onto (41) therefore meets the theorem’s tube condition                                  24

25   under the declared arithmetic contract, regardless of errors in the neural proposal. 25
26   The proof does not verify the interpreter or processor. The arithmetic assump- 26
27   tions, overflow checks, saved weights, and input-clock convention remain explicit                                      27

28   components of the computational claim.                                                                                 28

                        6. EARLIER LEARNING AND EXPERIMENTAL RECORD                                28

29     The following learning specification and study are retained from the reviewed               29

30   manuscript. Their fitted policies and numerical protocols are distinct from the               30

31   complete population and direct-method study reported in the main article and at               31

32   the beginning of this supplement.                                                             32
                                         NBO SUPPLEMENT                                      137

1                       7. BELLMAN EVALUATION WITH COSTATE TARGETS                                  1

2                                                                                                   2
       The post-training certificate applies to any admissible candidate; it does not select
3                                                                                                   3
     a numerical method in advance. We therefore change the evaluation experiment
4                                                                                                   4
     as well as the verification step. The purpose is to determine whether a separately
5                                                                                                   5
     trained continuation value and feasible actor produce economically useful state
6                                                                                                   6
     dependence, rather than merely remain inside a safe architectural interval.
7                                                                                                   7
       For a frozen policy a( j) , a positive-time Euler rollout from (t, y) supplies a sample
8           b ( j) (t, y) and its pathwise derivative pb( j) (t, y). Differentiation includes the   8
     return V
9                                                                                                   9
     state dependence of subsequent policy actions. Freezing the policy means freezing
10                                                                                                  10
     its parameters, not deleting that derivative. The targets evaluate the current policy,
11                                                                                                  11
     not the optimal policy, and contain no maximizing action labels. For fixed finite
12
     weights, bounded actions and smooth bounded production justify pathwise dif- 12
13                                                                                                  13
     ferentiation of this finite rollout away from the harmless clipping kinks. These are
14                                                                                                  14
     derivatives of the discretized evaluation problem, not asserted unbiased derivatives
15                                                                                                  15
     of its continuous-time limit.
16                                                                                                  16
       The critic minimizes
17                                                                                                  17
                           h                                  i
                                      ( j) 2           ( j) 2
18                       E .05{vθ − V } + ∥d( Dy vθ − p )∥d .
                         b          b                 b                                      (38)   18

19                                                                                                  19
     The factor d puts normalized marginal values on an order-one scale. The critic
20                                                                                                  20
     has the same two hidden tanh layers and hard terminal component as the R10
21                                                                                                  21
     implementation. Holding its new derivative fixed, the actor then maximizes
22                                                                                                  22
                              h                                i
23
                           E
                           b log aϕ − ζ ā2ϕ − aϕ · sg( Dy vθ ) .
                                       2                                                     (39)   23

24                                                                                                  24

25   Terms independent of actions have been omitted. Consumption is represented                     25

26   directly by m0 (t) + ϵ tanh Nϕ (t, y); there is no online Hamiltonian search in its            26

27   deployment. Value evaluation, costate evaluation, and feasible improvement are                 27

28   distinct operations, with their own stored losses and work counts. Five critic steps           28

29   and five actor steps are performed for each fresh rollout batch. Failure of either             29

30   numerical block is recorded, not interpreted as successful policy improvement.                 30

31     The stochastic gradient targets make the economically relevant marginal continu- 31
32   ation values visible to the evaluation loss. This is not a new Bellman principle, nor          32
     138

1    an assumption that derivative regression invariably dominates differential residual     1

2    fitting. It is a concrete implementation of the NBO evaluation map. The preserved       2

3    differential implementation and its two independent trace banks continue to test a      3

4    different evaluation procedure. Both feed the same economic distinction between         4

5    policy evaluation and feasible improvement.                                             5

6                                                                                            6

7                                 7.1. Comparison and selection                              7

8      The primary comparisons include direct policy optimization through the same           8

9    simulator and an affine state-dependent actor followed by tanh and the same feasi- 9
10   bility guard. Direct policy optimization has the same nonlinear actor architecture      10

11   but no learned critic; the affine method exploits a simpler representation. They        11

12   receive the same mixture of training states, the same seeds, the same rollout length,   12

13   and the same number of simulation-state visits. Their optimizer-step counts differ      13

14   by design and are reported, as are wall time and deployment cost. Equality of           14

15   simulator work is not described as equality of total computation.                       15

16     Every twentieth iteration is saved. Each method chooses the checkpoint with           16

17   the highest return on its own fixed validation bank before any final-test shocks are    17

18   examined. The archive includes all six checkpoints, not only the selected one. Longer   18

19   direct-policy and affine runs expose whether a short baseline budget is responsible     19

20   for a comparison. Cost–accuracy frontiers use the saved checkpoints and the same        20

21   final statistical accounting. The method is not declared superior merely because one    21

22   direct greedification of an inaccurate critic performs poorly.                          22

23     Training and validation use finite Euler rollouts. Verification instead uses the spec- 23
24   ified sampled controller, an independent fine-grid paired statistic, and Proposition 8. 24
25   An empirical rollout improvement is therefore not substituted for the continuous- 25
26   time conclusion. Conversely, no uniform HJB residual is assumed merely because a        26

27   rollout or costate loss is small.                                                       27

28                                                                                           28

29
                 8. THE ECONOMIC VALUE OF STATE-DEPENDENT CONSUMPTION                        29

30     The quantitative question is whether conditioning consumption on the evolving,        30

31   densely coupled capital state improves upon a common feasible consumption plan          31

32   enough to justify the computational intervention. This question is posed in the         32
                                         NBO SUPPLEMENT                                     139

1    original benchmark economy; the exercise is not a calibration to household or                 1

2    firm data. The relevant comparison is the schedule itself, not just another neural            2

3    implementation. All reported gains are differences in the original discounted utility         3

4    criterion.                                                                                    4

5                                                                                                  5

6                                                                                                  6
                                 8.1. Design and primary evidence
7                                                                                                  7

8      The final design uses ten independent training seeds in each of dimensions ten,             8

9    twenty, and fifty. Each seed supplies an NBO actor, a direct-policy actor, and an             9

10   affine actor. The parameter vector and dense matrix-generation rule are unchanged             10

11   from R10. The primary candidate architecture has width 32, two hidden layers,                 11

12   radius .1, and 120 outer iterations. Each selected policy is evaluated from y =               12

13   0 using 8,192 fresh paths and 1,024 time cells. A family allocation of 2,000 one- 13
14   sided statements at total probability .05 covers the primary comparisons and all              14

15   declared sensitivity rows. The final noise seed differs from the exploratory bank             15

16   used during development. This is a design fixed before the final remote computation,          16

17   not preregistration before all research.                                                      17

18                                                                                                 18

1      Of the 30 primary NBO policies, 30 have a strictly positive simultaneous lower          1

2    improvement endpoint. The lower endpoints range from 0.000716 to 0.001193 in              2

3    discounted utility units. The number meeting the declared improvement target .0005        3

4    is 30. The largest policy-specific NBO regret upper endpoint is 0.026672. These are       4

5    results for the stated implementation, initial state, and conditional sampling contract. 5
6    The archive contains 230 two-sided evaluation records and 0 logged training failures;     6

7    an unmet gain target is recorded separately from a training exception.                    7

8      The table reports the range across fitted seeds, including the least favourable seed,   8

9    rather than a confidence interval obtained after averaging fitted actors within each      9

10   path. The family-wise endpoints describe simulation uncertainty conditional on            10

11   each fitted object. The accompanying seed standard deviations describe algorithmic        11

12   variability; they are not asserted to estimate the distribution of every possible train- 12
13   ing run. The raw seed-by-path outcomes permit both quantities to be recomputed. 13

9                                                                                                9
       The feasible schedule has zero training cost. Its deterministic regret bound is
10                                                                                               10
     printed beside the policy-specific endpoints. The reported verification cost includes
11                                                                                               11
     fine-grid simulation and enclosure construction; it is not hidden in a training-only
12                                                                                               12
     runtime. Once a policy has been verified, deployment requires the stored actor and
13                                                                                               13
     an internal state update, but no critic optimization or global action search. Machine
14                                                                                               14
     timing is a property of the recorded CPU run, not a dimension-free complexity
15                                                                                               15
     theorem.
16                                                                                               16
       The archived R10 actors had negative actor-minus-schedule payoff differences in
17                                                                                               17
     dimensions twenty and fifty. Those outcomes are retained. They are not relabelled as
18                                                                                               18
     results of the new rollout-costate implementation. The current comparison changes
19                                                                                               19
     policy evaluation and specifies sampled deployment; the new continuous-time
20                                                                                               20
     transfer bound accounts for the latter. It does not isolate every source of the differ-
21                                                                                               21
     ence from R10, and it does not turn a method-specific experiment into a universal
22                                                                                               22
     ranking of actor–critic and direct methods.
23                                                                                               23
       Checkpoint frontiers and extended-budget baselines make improvement per unit
24                                                                                               24
     of work visible. A larger actor class has to earn its extra cost relative to the affine
25                                                                                               25
     policy and the direct-policy method. These comparisons are retained even when a
26                                                                                               26
     simpler method is competitive. All final policies, losses, failures, selected iterations,
27                                                                                               27
     and peak memory records appear in the replication archive.

19   log-flow supplement holding the reference resource allocation and other payoff               19

20   terms fixed. It is not a self-financing change in consumption and is not an estimated        20

21   welfare percentage for a calibrated population. A positive lower gain also bounds            21

22   an admissible additional implementation charge in utility units. This interpretation         22

23   makes both the magnitude of the learned benefit and its practical limits visible.            23

24     The validation-tuned critic-greedy comparisons, including the explicitly projected         24

25   direct-full hybrid, appear in the supplement.                                                25

26     The supplement reports radii .05, .10, and .15; the first and third rescale a frozen       26

27   fitted correction and are not represented as retraining at those radii. Widths 16 and        27

28   64 are separately trained. Initial-state tests include shifted means, greater dispersion,    28

29   and a combined shift–dispersion stress. The exact initial vectors, action saturation,        29

30   and fraction of internal states outside the training box are saved. Global feasibility       30

31   alone is not evidence of useful extrapolation. The statistical statements remain             31

32   pointwise at these declared states, not uniform over every state between them.               32

15                                                                                                   15
                        8.4. Connection to the other economic applications
16                                                                                                   16

17     Recursive utility, endogenous preference formation, temporal selves, and dy- 17
18   namic games remain applications of the NBO evaluation–improvement distinction,                  18

19   not consequences of the additive-noise capital theorem. Their full model equations,             19

20   original calculations, counterexamples, tables, and proofs have been relocated with- 20
21   out deletion to the supplement. The recursive problem requires its utility-domain               21

22   and comparison conditions. The preference certificates refer to their specified nodal           22

23   and continuous-action objects, with continuous-state, time, and stopping transfer               23

24   still separately accounted for. The strategic results bound deviations in the saved             24

25   finite dynamic games. The sophisticated-self problem uses its own equilibrium                   25

26   deviation condition.                                                                            26

27     This organization preserves the original research program while placing one                   27

28   complete computation and its new policy-specific guarantee at the centre of the                 28

29   article. Neither the broader title nor the retention of those applications enlarges the         29

30   scope of Theorem 9. The final source records a byte-verified relocation map and                 30

31   exact pre-revision roots, so the change in exposition can be distinguished from a               31

32   change in scientific content.                                                                   32
